#5053. $50K next for Bitcoin? Examining whether this will be its ‘worst’ Q4 since 2018

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Is Bitcoin’s worst Q4 on record getting closer?

The last 24 hours may have served as a reality check for investors. Bitcoin broke below the $62,000-level for the first time since August. However, the sharp decline may not be an isolated move. The sell-off followed the release of the latest FOMC minutes, adding to concerns over the Fed’s next policy move.

So, with Q4 already shaping up to be one of Bitcoin’s weakest quarters, could the worst be yet to come?

The latest FOMC minutes could be an early warning sign for Bitcoin’s Q4 outlook.

According to the minutes, almost all Fed officials now expect more rate hikes to bring inflation back to the 2% target. Some even suggested that a 50-basis-point hike could be on the table if inflation data worsens. In this context, Bitcoin’s latest correction suggests that investors may be taking a bearish view of the report. It is set to lay the tone for the next Fed meeting, scheduled for 10th December.

In short, the Fed maintained interest rates at 3.75%-4% in October, but the minutes have now strengthened expectations of another December hike. Here, it’s worth pointing out that market expectations already reflect this shift.

According to CME data, for instance, the odds of a rate hike have reached 100%. Of this, 92.7% of traders expect a 25-basis-point hike, taking rates to 4%-4.25%. The remaining 7.3% expect a bigger, 50-basis-point hike to 4.25%-4.5%.

Meanwhile, the pressure is also showing up in traditional markets.

The Dollar Index rose to 103.82 on 18th November, its highest level since April 2025. As a result, yields on U.S 10-year Treasuries rose above 5.1%. Also, it seems increasingly likely that if this continues, traditional assets will become more appealing than riskier assets. This is likely due to the hawkish tone of the FOMC minutes which said,

Almost all participants judged that further action to raise the target range for the federal funds rate would likely be appropriate.

Bitcoin’s [BTC] Q4 decline is getting closer to its 2018 record now.

As the chart below highlights, Bitcoin is down 24.45% in Q4 2026, with November’s 18.48% decline accelerating the correction. This puts BTC on track for one of its weakest fourth quarters on record. The closest historical comparison is Q4 2018, when Bitcoin fell by 43.5% during the bear market.

Sure, the prevailing decline is still 19.05% short of that record. However, this is where the latest FOMC minutes come into play. The release has added to concerns across the crypto market, with traders now weighing the possibility of further rate hikes.

If these expectations continue to rise, Bitcoin could face more selling pressure, pushing its Q4 decline closer to the 2018 record.

However, the bigger question is whether this selling pressure can continue or not.

With rate hikes now almost fully priced in, the focus will shift to whether Bitcoin has enough demand to absorb further selling. This would make on-chain activity and institutional flows key indicators to watch. At the time of writing though, the signals were not encouraging.

In fact, according to a recent Wintermute report,

With flows negative and the 200-week lost, December Fed hike is priced at about 92% after core CPI printed 0.3% for a third month, and spot Bitcoin ETFs are $1.9B net negative for 2026. We expect pressure into the $50s, with Saturday’s $62K low the first level and the $60K put cluster on Deribit the next magnet.

To understand how the macro environment could affect Bitcoin’s Q4 performance, the focus should also be on key on-chain data.

According to Glassnode’s latest report, Bitcoin has been facing strong selling pressure. Investors are booking losses at levels last seen near major market bottoms in 2018, 2020 and 2022. However, the $62K-66K range is acting as a key support zone, with nearly 20% of short-term holder supply sitting in this range.

That’s not all though. According to CryptoQuant, Bitcoin’s 30-day Net Realized Profit/Loss dropped to -$8.4 billion recently, its lowest level since the summer. Selling has accelerated too, with investors realizing $5.4 billion in losses between 15th-18th November alone. Even long-term holders have been selling at a loss, with their SOPR falling to 0.73 — its lowest level since August.

The key takeaway? The ongoing losses are still lower than those seen during the February (30 billion) and June (18.4 billion) bottoms. This means that Bitcoin may not have reached full capitulation “yet.”

So, if Bitcoin falls below $60K, this could lead to liquidations of nearly 20% of the short-term supply.

Considering the hawkish Fed, bearish technicals, and negative on-chain signals, a further downtrend cannot be ruled out. In fact, Wintermute now anticipates a further decline, with $50K now becoming a possible downside target for BTC.

The comparison with gold adds another layer to the picture.

From a technical perspective, Bitcoin has been flashing signs of some positive momentum relative to gold. On 18th November, the Bitcoin-to-Gold rate rose above its 200-day simple moving average to 17.13 ounces of gold for one Bitcoin. However, the broader trend might tell us a different story.

Despite this recovery, however, the BTC/XAU ratio was still down nearly 16% in Q4 at press time.

In other words, while Bitcoin has bounced against gold in the short term, it continues to underperform over the quarter. So, for long-term holders, gold still has the upper hand, while Bitcoin needs a stronger recovery to close the performance gap.

This finding also brings the cautious FOMC minutes back into focus.

Bitcoin now needs stronger spot demand, ETF inflows and a recovery in the BTC/XAU ratio to limit further losses. However, ongoing capitulation raises concerns about whether buyers can absorb the pressure. With the next FOMC meeting adding to market uncertainty, Wintermute’s $50K downside target will be in play.

Technically, that would pull Bitcoin nearly 40% lower in Q4. If selling pressure continues, the possibility of Bitcoin’s Q4 correction surpassing 2018’s record cannot be ruled out.

Bitcoin’s Q4 decline hit 24.45%, with weak on-chain data, ETF outflows, and hawkish Fed expectations adding to selling pressure.

A drop to $50K could push Bitcoin’s Q4 losses close to 40%, putting 2018’s record within reach.

#5052. Senators want state-licensed firms to keep issuing stablecoins after Jan. 18

Six senators are asking Treasury to let qualifying issuers keep issuing stablecoins while federal regulators review their states’ rules. The GENIUS Act takes effect Jan. 18, 2027.

Six senators are asking Treasury to let qualifying state-regulated stablecoin issuers keep putting new tokens into circulation while their states’ rules are under federal review. The GENIUS Act takes effect on January 18, 2027. Treasury’s proposal recognizes temporary relief for eligible federal applicants that get a waiver. It leaves open what happens if a state’s regulatory framework still awaits certification.

Sens. Cynthia Lummis and Kirsten Gillibrand led the bipartisan November 17 letter, which Lummis’s office released November 18. They asked Treasury for temporary protection during certification for issuers in states that will have a qualifying framework in place before January 18, 2028. They warned that a clearer federal route could steer issuers toward a federal license.

The proposed Section 3 rule would recognize relief for two groups with applications pending when the law takes effect. One is a subsidiary of an insured depository institution whose parent has applied on its behalf. The other is an applicant seeking to become a federally qualified payment stablecoin issuer. The primary federal regulator must grant a waiver, and its terms must cover the relevant Section 3 restriction. Relief can run for up to 12 months from the law’s effective date.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) has a separate final rule that requires a written request and a substantially complete issuer application pending on or before January 18, 2027. It can grant relief on public-interest grounds or in extraordinary circumstances. Its licensing page says it has yet to open applications or waiver requests and is awaiting clearance for its application forms.

Treasury’s proposal treats the first transfer that gives someone outside the issuer the right to use, transfer or redeem the token as issuance. An issuer could create tokens for its own inventory and still face a restriction when it tries to put those tokens into circulation.

The OCC waiver rule gives active issuers a route to request temporary relief before the January deadline.

Treasury asked whether issuers should get relief before a state files for certification or while a review is pending. Four state regulatory and legislative groups asked for such protection in October. New York’s Department of Financial Services offered a different reading. It says qualifying issuers can keep issuing if their state’s rules are substantially similar and the state files within a year, until any final denial. It wants Treasury to confirm that reading in the final rule.

In the preamble to its interim rule, the Stablecoin Certification Review Committee described the state option for issuers with $10 billion or less in consolidated outstanding stablecoin issuance as requiring a state filing and committee approval. The committee has 30 days to decide once a complete certification has been submitted. It will seek to expedite initial reviews after the Act takes effect for states that had a qualifying regime in place by January 14, 2026. New York says it expects to qualify for expedited review. The initial state filing is due within a year of the Act taking effect.

The committee says it will start accepting certifications once the forms clear federal review. The paperwork record still shows the certification forms under review. If Treasury treats committee approval as a prerequisite and offers no temporary relief, issuers that depend on the state route could have to wait to issue new units.

Treasury’s proposal recognizes express federal waivers. It leaves the treatment of issuers under pending state certifications open.

A January pause could affect new supply before the general platform-sales rule applies.

Foreign-issued stablecoins face separate platform requirements from January 18, 2027.

The two branches map the federal waiver in proposed § 1523.4(a) and Treasury’s question about relief while state certification is pending. New York’s legal reading comes from its October 5 comment. The dates are statutory milestones.

Foreign-issued stablecoins face separate platform requirements from January 18, 2027.

New York’s approvals show why the legal issuer matters. The state’s Greenlist and charter register identify Ripple USD (RLUSD) and WisdomTree Dollar (USDW) as approved for issuance in the state. Ripple’s November 16 terms name Ripple Custody & Trust Co., the trade name of New York-chartered Standard Custody & Trust Company, LLC, as the RLUSD issuer.

The OCC charter record shows that Ripple National Trust Bank’s charter became effective October 26. Ripple said Standard Custody would continue issuing RLUSD, with the separate national bank authorized to manage reserves and provide custody.

WisdomTree identifies a different New York trust, WisdomTree Digital Trust Company, LLC, as USDW’s issuer. For either coin, a federal waiver would have to cover issuance by the legal issuer.

ZUSD still appears on the Greenlist. Its issuer, GMO-Z.com Trust Company, Inc., removed the mint function in May during a voluntary wind-down. The issuer said direct redemptions ended November 11 and that remaining balances are subject to mandatory redemption. ZUSD’s wind-down was already underway months before Treasury published its proposal.

Under the GENIUS Act, January 18, 2027 is the effective date for the U.S. issuance restriction. If a state-regulated issuer has to pause, the immediate effect would be on its ability to supply fresh units to customers and distributors. Trading already-issued tokens and redeeming them with the issuer are separate activities.

Starting July 18, 2028, digital asset service providers such as exchanges generally can offer or sell payment stablecoins to people in the U.S. only if the issuer is permitted or an exception applies.

A separate foreign-issuer provision applies from the Act’s effective date. It conditions U.S. platform availability on the foreign issuer’s ability and commitment to comply with lawful orders and reciprocal arrangements. Section 18 also provides a route for foreign issuers from comparable regimes that register with the OCC and meet other conditions.

#5051. Investor Bill Miller Says Bitcoin Is Easier to Value than AI

Famous Bitcoin (BTC) advocate and investor Bill Miller IV said that it is much easier to value the world's largest cryptocurrency than AI investments, even though Bitcoin does not produce any cash flow.

He said that this has to do with the expectations investors have when it comes to these two assets, specifically regarding their capacity to deliver future profits.

In a recent X post, Miller, who is the chairman and chief investment officer of Miller Value Partners, broke down why he thinks that it's easier to make an investment case for Bitcoin than for AI companies.

"Bitcoin and AI owners have different expectations. The owners of AI expect companies will one day produce free cash flow. Bitcoin owners never did or will expect this," Miller said.

And the end result is that "the valuation case is still much easier for Bitcoin than AI."

Simply put, when investors buy stocks in AI companies, there is an expectation that at some point these companies will deliver big profits and free cash flow.

Free cash flow is the money a company produces from its operations after capital expenditures. This is a significant metric for understanding the value of a company.

However, when it comes to AI companies, it can be difficult to assign a value because investors need to try to determine what kind of revenue these companies will generate, how much they will invest in infrastructure, and when these investments will start to deliver sufficient returns.

Miller has made it a point to push back against the argument that Bitcoin does not have value because it doesn't produce cash flow.

In a commentary from June 30, Miller explained that the traditional way of looking at intrinsic value, which revolves around future cash flows, doesn't really capture the potential of Bitcoin as a decentralized monetary network.

Instead, Miller said that Bitcoin's value could be determined by comparing its potential role within the global financial system to that of gold.

In his view, Bitcoin could theoretically reach about $1.4 million per coin if it achieved a valuation similar to gold's. At least, that's what he calculated at the time.

#5050. Quantum Upgrade Could Be 'Particularly Challenging' for Blockchains: IOSCO

Quantum Upgrade Could Be 'Particularly Challenging' for Blockchains: IOSCO

Financial markets, including crypto, need to start preparing now for quantum computers capable of breaking the cryptography that secures them, according to the world's main forum for securities regulators.

A "sufficiently capable" quantum computer could undermine widely used public-key cryptography that supports critical functions across financial markets, the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) said in a report published on Thursday. The cyber readiness challenge posed by quantum computing "is immediate and requires action," it said.

"Practical quantum computers capable of compromising widely used cryptography do not yet exist, but the scale and duration of the transition mean the financial sector should start preparing today," IOSCO Board Chair Jean-Paul Servais said in a statement.

The 63-page report cited conservative estimates that such machines could emerge between 2030 and 2035. Several industry participants consulted by IOSCO went further, arguing that firms should prepare for the possibility that they arrive within five years, potentially accelerated by AI.

IOSCO said the migration may be particularly challenging for blockchains, because cryptography is embedded in their core architecture and any change requires coordinated action across decentralized networks. Public blockchains such as Ethereum, Solana and Tron rely heavily on elliptic curve cryptography to verify identities and validate transactions, and a powerful enough quantum computer could derive private keys from exposed public keys, a scenario known as "Q-Day."

Swapping in quantum-resistant signatures comes at a cost. Signatures under ML-DSA-44, a post-quantum standard from the U.S. National Institute of Standards and Technology, are about 2,420 bytes, roughly 35 to 40 times larger than today's 64-byte elliptic curve signatures, the report said. It cited evidence from a quantum-resistant Solana testnet that saw throughput fall by about 90% when post-quantum signatures replaced existing ones.

Because the cryptography sits in transaction formats and consensus rules, replacing it requires hard forks, and decentralized networks have no way to force quick adoption, making migration "slow and fragmented," IOSCO said. Accounts and smart contracts still controlled by vulnerable keys could stay exposed after a network adds quantum-resistant options, and the path to quantum resistance for Bitcoin wallets remains uphill.

Wallet makers face their own trade-offs. SHRINCS, the leading candidate for a post-quantum Bitcoin signature scheme, offers "stateful" signatures that are about 10 times smaller but can expose funds to theft if keys are reused beyond a set signing budget, Trezor CTO Tomáš Sušánka has said. Trezor is considering making the larger, safer stateless option the default, and hardware wallets will also need post-quantum cryptography for firmware and device authentication, he added.

The report distinguished "on-spend" attacks, in which an attacker races to exploit a public key revealed by a pending transaction, from "at-rest" attacks on keys left exposed for longer. Dormant or abandoned coins pose a particular problem where their holders can't move them. Crypto firms have formed a quantum alliance as some 6 million BTC sit in exposed addresses, and Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has proposed "quantum landmines" to make attacks on such coins costly.

Layer-2 networks are not insulated either. Optimistic rollups could face forged signatures during their dispute windows, typically seven days on Ethereum, and many zero-knowledge rollups use SNARK proofs that rely on elliptic curves. Hash-based STARK proofs are more resistant, but a rollup is only as safe as the layer-1 contracts, bridges and governance keys it depends on, the report said.

Developers are already working on fixes. Researchers have proposed several ways to make Bitcoin quantum-safe, StarkWare has shown a quantum-safe Bitcoin transaction, and Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has said the network is betting its future on quantum resistance.

IOSCO said firms should migrate to post-quantum cryptography and build "crypto-agility," the ability to update cryptographic systems without major redesign. Quantum key distribution could complement those measures on selected high-security channels, it added.

Most preparations across financial markets are still at an early stage, focused on awareness, governance, training and identifying vulnerable systems, according to the report, which drew on a March 2026 survey of members of IOSCO's Fintech Task Force and discussions with experts. Some respondents described "quantum-procrastination," in which firms put off action while waiting for clearer regulatory signals or wider adoption.

No surveyed jurisdiction reported operational deployment of quantum computing in regulated financial services. The watchdog also urged regulators to act against "quantum washing," in which firms overstate the role, maturity or performance of quantum technology. IOSCO said it will treat quantum as a priority in 2027, focusing on raising awareness across the financial ecosystem.

Quantum hardware is advancing quickly. IBM has claimed a trusted quantum advantage, and Google has said it has cracked some of the field's biggest bottlenecks.

#5047. Bithumb halts CYBER deposits and withdrawals while trading stays open

The South Korean exchange cited a suspected security issue and said it would credit deposits received during the halt in order once transfers resume.

Bithumb suspended deposits and withdrawals of the CYBER token after flagging signs of a possible security issue. The exchange said customers could still trade CYBER already held in their accounts. Kraken separately warned of possible delays for CYBER deposits sent over the Optimism network.

It also urged customers to take care when trading CYBER, saying the security concern could lead to sharper price swings.

The Nov. 21–22 exchange notices affected three different ways customers could use CYBER.

Bithumb said CYBER deposits received during the pause would be credited in sequence after service resumed.

The lanes show service status based on the dated exchange notices. Line lengths do not represent the duration of a pause or delay.

The lanes show service status based on the dated exchange notices. Line lengths do not represent the duration of a pause or delay.

#5044. Gen Z Embraces Crypto While Most Americans Call It a 'Side Quest'

Gen Z Embraces Crypto While Most Americans Call It a 'Side Quest'

For many young Americans, crypto is coming before the 401(k).

Some 21% of Gen Z adults own cryptocurrency, compared with 16% who hold an employer-sponsored retirement plan like a 401(k), according to a survey released Thursday by SoFi Technologies.

That’s seven times the rate of baby boomers, just 3% of whom own crypto. Crypto was Gen Z’s most widely held investment in the survey, ahead of stocks, bonds ,and real estate.

The online survey of 2,064 U.S. adults was conducted by SoFi and YouGov from Oct. 15 to 28. SoFi, a nationally chartered bank that sells crypto alongside checking and investment accounts, developed the report with Culture Co-op.

Gen Z respondents described crypto less as a gamble than a strategy. They were nearly three times as likely as the general population to call it one of the best ways to build future wealth, 21% vs. 8%, and 61% said they see it as a financial advantage unique to their generation.

Only 20% of Gen Z crypto owners said they bought in because it “sounded interesting,” compared with 34% of crypto investors overall. Diversification (32%) and easier international payments (29%) ranked higher.

Part of the gap reflects age. Many respondents in their late teens and 20s don’t yet have access to an employer retirement plan.

Across the broader population, the picture is far cooler. Only 13% of Americans own crypto, and 62% are firm skeptics. Three in four say crypto feels intimidating to get into, and 77% agreed with the statement, “Cryptocurrency feels like a financial side quest that I don’t have the time or energy for.”

Even owners feel out of their depth: 82% of current crypto investors said they should know more about it than they do, and 11% admitted knowing little to nothing.

“Nobody feels the gaps in their financial education more sharply than someone who has just bought Bitcoin,” said Natalie Brunell, host of the Coin Stories podcast and a contributor to SoFi’s crypto newsletter.

The findings echo a recent survey conducted by Ipsos for the DeFi Education Fund, which found that 22% of Americans have ever owned or used crypto but only 13% used it in the past year.

SoFi argues that putting crypto in the same app as banking is the key to wider adoption. Half of respondents open to crypto said having their primary bank offer it would be a tipping point, and 63% of current investors said they’d trade more if they could do it at their bank.

SoFi has been building out that model, launching a tokenized deposit that pays 4.4% and moves like a stablecoin, as well as a bank-issued stablecoin running on Polygon’s payments infrastructure.

The survey also challenges the crypto “tech bro” stereotype. Black and Hispanic respondents own crypto at more than twice the rate of white respondents, 22% to 9%. Miami led the 10 cities surveyed at 20% ownership, while San Francisco matched the national average at 13%.

#5042. Robert Kiyosaki Reveals Why He Is Buying Bitcoin and Ethereum

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Renowned financial guru and Rich Dad Poor Dad author Robert Kiyosaki has offered compelling insight into the reasons behind his buying spree of Bitcoin (BTC) and Ethereum (ETH) despite the current crypto market crash.

Kiyosaki, who is an esteemed investor, predicts that a financial crisis could be on the horizon, and it might be even worse than the 2008 market crash. He believes that crypto assets and other hard assets are an effective hedge against the repercussions of government-induced overborrowing and money printing.

The financial guru's latest X post explains his rationale behind investing in Bitcoin, Ethereum, gold, silver, real estate, and oil instead of traditional financial assets.

"Why I am buying real (no ETFs) gold, silver, Bitcoin, and Ethereum," Kiyosaki noted in his latest post.

He attributed his views to the Federal Reserve, the U.S. Treasury and the colossal debt obligations of the U.S.

According to Kiyosaki, the government's inability to curb spending will lead to massive money printing in the future. This might erode the purchasing power of the U.S. dollar.

"The BIG PRINT is about to begin," Kiyosaki warned.

This is the core reason for his investments in Bitcoin and Ethereum. He believes that these assets will rise in value when the government is forced to aggressively print money in response to escalating financial issues.

Moreover, he believes that holding these crypto assets directly would be better than investing in exchange-traded funds (ETFs). However, he did not offer a reason for his opposition to ETFs in his post.

Furthermore, Kiyosaki stated that the next financial crisis could be much bigger than the global financial crisis of 2008.

"This crash we are just now sliding into is much bigger than 2008. Possibly a global depression," he commented.

He also mentioned that his gold, silver, Bitcoin and Ethereum holdings could make him one of the ultra-rich if his predictions come true.

However, Kiyosaki mentioned some figures that were not entirely accurate. For instance, he stated that the U.S. national debt stood at $49 trillion. But according to Treasury data, the national debt was about $40.7 trillion in November 2026. Also, his claim that the U.S. is printing $1 trillion every 90 days conflates government debt with money printing.

Although Kiyosaki is a strong believer in Bitcoin, he recently advised investors not to buy Bitcoin immediately amid the digital asset's current price slump.

On Nov. 20, the Rich Dad Poor Dad author cautioned against catching a 'falling knife.'

"Never grab a falling knife." This means that investors should wait for the market crash to end before buying.

"I started buying bitcoin around $20k after the market crashed last time," Kiyosaki recalled.

"Will wait for Bitcoin bottom again," he added.

#5039. AI banking agent scored 100% on its own tests then failed 92 of 97 real-world simulations

In a Uniphore benchmark, 13 Claude Opus 4.8 builds gained 16.9 percentage points on their own tests and 3.2 points on cases held back from them.

AI systems tasked with building business agents often gave their work near-perfect grades on tests they wrote themselves. In a Nov. 19 preprint, researchers at Uniphore found that the grades could improve far more than scores on cases withheld from the builders during development.

The researchers used AppSci-Bench, a benchmark that puts AI builders to work in seven simulated business workflows, including invoice review, customer relationship management and airline rebooking. A builder could ask a simulated business user what the agent needed to do, then develop it using the tools and data available in that setting.

In the test runs that allowed revisions, builders could write their own tests and keep changes that raised the score. After the builder submitted an agent, the benchmark ran it on separate cases withheld during development. The researchers released the benchmark code and recorded results alongside the preprint.

Uniphore researchers found that 86 of the 132 builds with a recorded self-test scored at least 99% on the tests their builders wrote. The researchers tracked 13 Claude Opus 4.8 builds that could revise their agents, comparing the first round of testing with the last. For those builds, scores rose an average of 16.9 points on tests the model wrote and 3.2 points on the withheld cases. In six of the 24 revision steps the researchers examined in that subset, the builder's grade rose while the withheld-case score held steady.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

AI builders could use their own tests to decide which changes to keep. AppSci-Bench also tested the submitted agents on cases withheld during development.

A large gain on the builder’s tests can coexist with a much smaller gain on unseen cases.

The evaluations used different cases and scoring rules. AppSci-Bench tested simulated business workflows.

The bars use the same zero-to-20 point scale. Both values are mean changes per build from first to final checkpoint in Table 3 of the Uniphore preprint.

The evaluations used different cases and scoring rules. AppSci-Bench tested simulated business workflows.

In a broader comparison of 252 builds using Claude Opus 5, Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5, the researchers found no statistically significant difference in withheld-case scores when they switched the test-and-revise loop on or off. That result held in both the researchers’ setup and the models’ native coding tools, Claude Code and Codex, after accounting for multiple comparisons.

One banking build showed how a test could reinforce a bad assumption. GPT-5.5 assumed it could not look customers up by email or name even though both options were available. One test it wrote rewarded asking for a customer ID, and the agent ended up refusing the other lookups. The agent eventually scored a perfect 1.0 on its builder’s tests. In the withheld trial the researchers examined, it succeeded on five of 97 cases.

Meta used a live search test after its AI agents screened candidate changes offline.

For a team evaluating a banking agent, a test that supplies only an email address could expose the same blind spot. Keeping a set of cases outside development lets the team see how the submitted agent handles situations its builder’s tests may never have covered.

#5037. Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic Tops SpaceX's Initial IPO With $81 Billion Deal: Where the Money Went

Anthropic has priced the largest initial IPO share sale in history at $81.34 billion, topping the $75 billion SpaceX first sold in June. But SpaceX still has the larger completed IPO after an additional share sale.

The Claude maker sold 980 million shares at $83 on Wednesday and is set to begin trading Thursday on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT.

The price beat the original $70-to-$78 range, while the company increased the offering from 825 million shares. Demand was about six times the number available before pricing.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perps on Decrypt.

At $83, Anthropic is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion including stock options and other potential shares, though its value based on outstanding shares is closer to $1.1 trillion.

The headline $81.34 billion isn't all money for Anthropic. About 956.35 million new shares bring $79.38 billion into the company before fees. Another 23.65 million shares, worth $1.96 billion, are being sold by its seven co-founders—roughly $280 million each. Anthropic receives none of that founders' cash, according to its final prospectus.

On top of the public sale, seven technology companies are buying $26 billion of nonvoting stock in a separate private placement. Google and Nvidia are putting in $10 billion each, AMD $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft $1 billion each, and TSMC $500 million.

The public offering and private placement total $107.34 billion in gross share sales, including the $1.96 billion going to founders rather than Anthropic.

The $104.8 billion net figure is for both transactions, not for the IPO alone. Anthropic gets $79.38 billion from its newly issued public shares, plus $26 billion from the private placement: $105.38 billion before costs. Subtract roughly $580 million in company-paid underwriting fees and estimated offering expenses, and the prospectus estimates $104.8 billion net.

That said, it could rise to about $117 billion if underwriters buy all the extra shares allowed. Anthropic plans to spend the money on operations, hiring and, above all, computing infrastructure.

Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley led a 22-bank underwriting group, with roughly $550 million in underwriting commissions. The company also offered access through brokers including Fidelity, Schwab, Robinhood, and SoFi, but retail investors received less than 20% of the offering.

Amazon remains the largest disclosed shareholder after the transaction with about 14.6% of the company, followed by Google at roughly 13.4%.

Early financial backers also hold enormous paper fortunes. Bloomberg estimates put Lightspeed's stake near $28 billion, Iconiq's above $26 billion and Menlo Ventures' around $22 billion. Ashton Kutcher's Sound Ventures reportedly holds shares worth almost $11 billion. Those figures are estimates of stock value, not cash they received at the IPO.

Investors, however, aren't buying much control. A special share class gives the founders' holding company 50.1% of shareholder voting power, while the Long-Term Benefit Trust can appoint a majority of the board.

So, Anthropic can raise historic sums while keeping key decisions largely away from ordinary shareholders.

Anthropic generated $33.24 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, including $16.96 billion in the third quarter alone—the latest reported quarter. Its $81 billion annualized revenue run rate at the end of October projects recent customer spending over a year; it isn't money already earned.

Roughly 250 organizations now spend at least $10 million annually with Anthropic, and more than 1,900 spend at least $1 million, using trailing 12-month figures. Its net revenue retention reached about 900%.

In plain English, the same eligible customer group spent nine times as much in the latest three-month period as it did a year earlier. That measure excludes fixed-price Claude subscriptions.

But the business still depends on a few major accounts and distribution partners. Its five biggest customers generated about 25% of revenue over the first nine months of 2026, while cloud marketplaces accounted for 44%. In other words, companies like Amazon, Google and Microsoft can invest in Anthropic, provide the machines it runs on and help sell access to Claude.

Even the product changes at extraordinary speed: Claude models released during the six months through September made up more than 80% of third-quarter revenue. At the end of September 2026, Anthropic had about 5,300 employees and was generating more than $6.6 million in trailing annual revenue per employee.

Rapid growth doesn't mean easy profits. Anthropic posted a $567 million operating loss in the third quarter of 2026, after a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter. Over the first nine months, its operating loss totaled $1.26 billion. Those numbers tell a very different story from its much bigger headline net loss.

In the first nine months of 2026, Anthropic posted a $104.04 billion net loss, including $94.3 billion in noncash revaluations of convertible financing and related instruments. Yet it generated $6.57 billion in cash from operating activities during the same period. That is not free cash flow, which also accounts for capital spending, but the distinction matters: a $104 billion accounting loss does not mean $104 billion of cash burned.

The latest balance sheet, dated September 30, 2026, shows $95.6 billion in cash and equivalents plus $36.3 billion in short-term investments—$131.85 billion combined. Convertible notes payable stood at $130.50 billion at their accounting value, with some converting into shares around the IPO.

The final prospectus estimates $232.35 billion in cash and short-term investments on a pro forma, post-transaction basis. That last figure is a modeled balance after the offering and related adjustments, not cash the company held on September 30.

The more consequential number may be what it has promised to spend next. Anthropic disclosed about $900.9 billion in commitments, overwhelmingly for computing and infrastructure, stretching as far as 2045.

It separately estimates another $150 billion to $175 billion in payments under arrangements without fixed dollar amounts. Together, that points to potentially more than $1 trillion—but it is not a bill due today, and some contracts are conditional or can be terminated.

Some $70.7 billion of the stated commitments fall due in the 12 months after September 2026. That explains why even an IPO this large is not necessarily excessive capital for a frontier AI company. Every breakthrough attracts customers, but serving them requires more chips, power, and data centers.

There is a technical catch to the record. SpaceX's June IPO initially sold $75 billion in shares, but the final deal reached $86.25 billion gross after underwriters bought more shares—about $85.675 billion after fees, according to SpaceX's SEC filing.

Anthropic's $81.34 billion beats SpaceX's initial deal, not its final gross total. The extra $26 billion from Anthropic's private placement cannot be counted as public IPO proceeds. Its own option covers another 147 million shares worth about $12.2 billion; if exercised in full, its public IPO would reach $93.54 billion, comfortably above SpaceX's final figure.

Now attention turns to OpenAI. The ChatGPT maker recently raised $11 billion at a reported $1.25 trillion valuation and has indicated it will not list before 2027. Anthropic's debut offers a public-market test of what investors are willing to pay for AI revenue, while exposing the cost of generating it.

The interesting part isn't just that a five-year-old AI lab topped SpaceX's initial IPO. It's that it assembled more than $100 billion in combined public and private financing, started generating cash from operations—and still faces a potential trillion-dollar computing bill.

#5035. Bitcoin Price Slips Near $61,500 as Tech Selloff Spreads to Asia

The bitcoin price slipped to near $61,500 on Friday morning, according to Look Into Bitcoin data, as a selloff in technology stocks that followed Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut spread to Asian markets and dragged bitcoin to its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin first broke below $62,000 on Thursday evening, after Thursday afternoon’s report showed it giving back a rebound toward $64,000. The bitcoin price then slid to nearly $61,200 during Asian trading before bouncing back above $61,700. Friday marks a third straight day of losses.

The selling started in Asia. South Korea’s Kospi slid more than 5%, with chipmakers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics down around 6%. The Korea Exchange halted program selling for five minutes after the drop, while Japan’s Nikkei fell as much as 4.85% to a seven-month low.

U.S. futures pointed lower too. Nasdaq-100 futures fell more than 1.7% and S&P 500 futures dropped 0.8%, Yahoo Finance reported. Gold futures lost 3%. The S&P 500 is pointing to a seventh straight daily decline, its longest losing streak since the start of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020.

Anthropic shares fell 8% in premarket trading after closing their first session at $97.17, up 17% from the $83 IPO price. Nvidia was down about 2.4% and AMD fell nearly 7%, CNBC reported. Hawkish signals from Federal Reserve officials this week also weighed on sentiment, Reuters said.

Bitcoin funds are feeling the pressure. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $541.1 million in net outflows on Thursday, its largest single-day outflow since launch. That topped the previous record of $528.3 million set on Jan. 30. The fund bled $2 billion over nine straight days of outflows earlier this month.

Leveraged traders took heavy losses as the bitcoin price broke support. Bitcoin liquidations hit $712.9 million over 24 hours, with $614 million coming from long positions. Open interest in bitcoin futures fell to $18 billion from $20 billion a week ago, and funding rates flipped broadly negative as traders unwound their bets.

Traders are now watching whether the bitcoin price can hold the $60,000 area. A large pocket of long positions sits in the $60,500 to $60,900 band, just below the current price, and could come into play on a further drop. Bitcoin is also on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, which would be its second-longest losing streak after nine weeks of declines in 2022.

Crypto stocks pointed lower in premarket trading. Strategy (MSTR) traded near $83.90, down about 2.6% from Thursday’s close of $86.13, after the stock fell for a sixth straight day on Thursday. Coinbase (COIN) slipped about 2.7% to near $106.

Bitcoin miners fell harder. IREN (IREN) dropped about 4.4%, CleanSpark (CLSK) lost about 3.6% and MARA Holdings (MARA) declined about 3.4%. Riot Platforms (RIOT) was down about 3.3%. IREN has also been under pressure as investors reassess AI infrastructure stocks. Delays in bringing some of its capacity online led Microsoft to spend less with the miner than planned last quarter.

Longer-term buyers have not left entirely. Institutional investors added 49,229 BTC through U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs in the third quarter, lifting their holdings to a record 356,491 BTC. Swiss bank Sygnum called the current downturn the shallowest bear market for bitcoin to date, putting the drawdown at around 54% compared with declines of more than 70% in previous cycles.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $61,800.

#5034. Claude-assisted proof of Riemann hypothesis validated by independent checkers

A clean rebuild reproduced the Lean proof export byte for byte, and separate tools checked its conclusion and reasoning.

Independent checkers validated Levent Alpöge’s Claude-assisted proof of the Riemann hypothesis, the Lean and Mathlib communities said on Nov. 21. Teams rebuilt his roughly 17-million-line library, matched its conclusion to the original conjecture and checked each step of the exported proof. They established what the code proved while a complete mathematical write-up was still to come.

The conjecture dates to 1859. It says that when the Riemann zeta function equals zero, the solutions other than a known set of exceptions all lie on the same vertical line, where the real part is one-half. Where those zeros lie helps explain how the distribution of prime numbers deviates from its average pattern.

Alpöge released the proof on Nov. 20 as code for Lean, a system for writing and checking mathematical proofs. In his release note, he said he chose a setting where two mathematical ideas could be combined and credited Anthropic’s AI assistant Claude with suggesting advances late in the argument.

In his MIT-hosted verification logs, Andrew Sutherland reported that a rebuild on a separate machine, with networking disabled and the source files set to read-only, produced a byte-for-byte match to the published proof export. The export contains the formal proof steps in a form that other checkers can read.

In their joint statement, the Lean and Mathlib communities said they put the target theorem in a separate challenge file that imported only Mathlib, Lean’s library of formalized mathematics. Two comparison tools confirmed that the conclusion in Alpöge’s proof matched Mathlib’s existing statement of the conjecture.

Two independently written checkers, nanoda and con-leche, accepted the full export, Sutherland reported. They checked whether the steps justified the conclusion using the exported data, which kept executable code in the original source files out of that part of the process.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

A clean rebuild matched the published export. Verifiers compared the result with Mathlib’s theorem statement and independently checked the proof steps.

The statement comparison identifies the theorem. The proof checks establish whether the steps justify it.

The diagram covers completed checks for the original conjecture. Clay’s decision follows its own publication and acceptance rules.

The left branch compares the submission with a separately prepared Mathlib challenge. The right branch checks the submitted proof export with two independent implementations.

The diagram covers completed checks for the original conjecture. Clay’s decision follows its own publication and acceptance rules.

Sutherland’s team also replayed all 175,187 compiled modules through Lean’s own checker. The team’s additional Lean runs on the raw export were still underway in his Nov. 22 update. The checks also covered a generalized version of the conjecture for Dirichlet L-functions.

Alpöge said he planned to write up the proof for mathematicians to read. Michael Rubinstein at the University of Waterloo posted a working guide dated Nov. 21 that explains parts of the argument for the original conjecture. The guide still relies on the Lean development for the proof of a central inequality.

Clay Mathematics Institute’s problem page still listed the conjecture as unsolved when checked on Nov. 22. Under Clay’s prize rules, the institute considers a proposed solution only after publication in a qualifying outlet, at least two years since that publication and general acceptance in the global mathematics community.

Anthropic Stock Opens 33% Above IPO Price as AI Stocks Rally

Initial indications on the IPO open climbed from $108 earlier in the session, with successive price estimates reportedly moving closer together, Yahoo reported.

During the opening process, market makers and underwriters monitor incoming buy and sell orders to determine a price at which supply and demand can meet. Indications quickly climbed as Nasdaq established its official opening price.

#5030. Michael Saylor sees up to $7.5 billion in ‘natural demand’ for Strategy’s STRC preferred stock

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Saylor said the credit-market contraction began around May, and the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level.

Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said "natural, organic" demand for the company's STRC preferred stock at its current 12% dividend rate is likely between $5 billion and $7.5 billion.

"I don't know exactly where the number is, but I’m going to go out on a limb and say that somewhere between $5 billion and $7.5 billion, there is natural organic demand to hold the instrument at 12% dividend," Saylor said during a live investor Q&A on Thursday.

His comments come as Strategy has been actively repurchasing STRC shares at prices below its $100 stated level. President and CEO Phong Le said the company has bought back about $3 billion of the preferred stock at prices between $95 and $99, below the issuance price level.

Saylor described the current environment as a credit-market contraction that began around May. He said the company is responding by reducing the amount of STRC outstanding until it reaches an equilibrium level that the market will support at the existing dividend rate.

"We're going to contract the outstanding STRC because 12% is a fat dividend for an investment-grade instrument,” Saylor said. "As we buy it back, we lengthen the duration, we strip the risk, we improve the credit."

"When the company is swapping either BTC or MSTR for STRC, we're supporting the credit, we're supporting the brand of the company, we're improving the product quality," Saylor said.

The Strategy Chairman also reiterated that the company has at least three years' worth of cash right now, so it will still be able to operate even if the current bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) downturn continues.

The world's largest digital asset treasury firm owned 850,849 BTC as of Nov. 15, according to its latest disclosure, which amounts to more than 4% of bitcoin's maximum supply cap. Last month, holders of Strategy's STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shares approved a change allowing dividends to accrue daily, rather than on the previous schedule.

Last week, the company sold approximately $244.7 million in MSTR shares to fund bitcoin purchases and STRC repurchases.

#5029. Bitcoin Hovers Just Above Average Holder’s Cost Basis as Price Keeps Dipping

Bitcoin is trading just above its realized price, the average cost of every coin on the network, and a break below that level would put the average holder at a loss for the first time since early 2023, Glassnode, an onchain analytics firm posted Friday.

The price of bitcoin sat near $61,700 on Friday after sliding below $62,000. The realized price is a measure of what holders paid, not where the market trades. When bitcoin trades above it, the typical coin is sitting on a gain. Below it, the typical coin is underwater.

The margin is thin. The firm said the MVRV ratio, which compares bitcoin’s market value with its realized value, sits “just above 1.” A reading of 1 means the market price has fallen all the way to the realized price.

Realized price values each coin at the price it last moved on the blockchain, then averages across the supply. Look Into Bitcoin describes it as bitcoin’s average cost basis. Because it tracks the price when coins last changed hands, it moves far more slowly than the market.

The MVRV ratio expresses the same idea as a multiple. Above 1, holders are in profit on average. Below 1, they are not. A reading just above 1 means the market price is only a sliver higher than what the average coin cost.

The firm did not give the exact realized price in its post, which included a chart. That leaves the exact cushion between today’s price and the average cost basis unstated. Bitcoin Magazine Pro’s full realized price chart sits behind a login, so the current level could not be confirmed independently for this report.

“The average Bitcoin holder is still in profit,” the firm wrote. “The margin is thin.”

The slide has come with heavy selling from funds. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost about $541 million on Thursday, its largest one-day outflow since it launched in January 2024, Bloomberg reported. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total that day.

The selling stands out against the buying earlier in the fall. Institutions piled into bitcoin ETFs through the third quarter, pushing their combined holdings to a record, Bitcoin Magazine reported this week.

Leveraged traders took losses too. More than $700 million in leveraged crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, and about 86% of them were bets on higher prices. Bitcoin accounted for roughly $200 million of the total.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said the drop is part of a longer downturn rather than the start of a new one. “I see this as a continuation of the existing bear market rather than the start of another,” he said in a note dated Nov. 18.

The note frames Friday’s price action as a late stage of a decline that began well before November. Butterfill pointed to whale selling that weighed on prices from late 2025, expectations of higher interest rates and weakness in technology stocks, all of which have kept buyers cautious.

“Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said. “Geopolitics will have a considerable say in that.”

A move through the realized price would put bitcoin near the bottom edge of that range. The firm said holders as a group have not been in loss since early 2023.

A sustained breakout, Butterfill said, would likely require “a change in the policy outlook, renewed whale participation or both.”

#5028. Repaying a Bitcoin-backed loan on Sui can leave you waiting to withdraw native BTC

Fluid borrowers reclaim hBTC after repayment. Redeeming that token for native Bitcoin goes through Hashi, which limits the pace and size of BTC withdrawals.

Once a Fluid borrower has repaid their USDC loan and reclaimed eligible hBTC collateral, they hold a Bitcoin-backed token on Sui. To get native Bitcoin into their wallet, they need a separate redemption from Hashi, the protocol that issues hBTC. Hashi’s payout limit can hold up an approved withdrawal even when the pool has enough BTC to cover it.

Fluid announced its hBTC lending markets on Nov. 20, a day after the Sui Foundation announced the market was live. Bitcoin holders can deposit BTC with Hashi, receive hBTC on the Sui network and pledge that token to borrow USDC, the first stablecoin available through the market.

The underlying coins remain on Bitcoin at an address derived for the depositor’s Sui account. Hashi’s deposit guide describes who can spend them in the normal withdrawal process. Validators in Hashi’s committee provide one authorization collectively, and a separate signer called the Guardian provides the other. Once the borrower has the hBTC back on Sui, those signers still have to authorize the Bitcoin payout.

Once a borrower has the hBTC back, they can request a withdrawal on Sui by submitting the amount and a compatible Bitcoin address. Hashi sets the hBTC aside and queues the request while it waits for a quorum of committee members to approve the request. The committee leader gathers approved requests into a batch so several users can receive Bitcoin in a single transaction.

Hashi burns the hBTC when the leader commits the batch. The committee jointly produces one signature for each Bitcoin input the batch spends. The Guardian checks the batch against its limit and supplies a second signature for each input. Once the transaction is fully signed, the leader broadcasts it, with each user’s share of the miner fee deducted from their payout. Hashi marks the withdrawal confirmed after the transaction reaches its required number of Bitcoin confirmations.

The Guardian’s withdrawal limit has two settings. One sets the maximum amount the system can release at once. The other determines how quickly capacity refills after a payout batch uses it. The pool balance measures the Bitcoin held by Hashi, while the limit governs how much of that Bitcoin can move at a given time.

If an approved request is within the maximum but is too large for the capacity available at that moment, it waits for room to open up. Requests behind it can wait too. Hashi skips requests above the maximum until governance raises the limit. The borrower can cancel and recover their hBTC after a cooldown, up until the leader commits the request to a batch.

The result depends on how much capacity the Guardian has available and whether the request fits the configured maximum.

Hashi can hold enough BTC even when the Guardian has too little payout capacity for a particular request.

The live maximum, refill rate and elapsed withdrawal times remain unverified.

The diagram maps the queue outcomes in Hashi’s documented withdrawal lifecycle and Guardian rate limiter. The three branches illustrate rules and use no numerical scale.

The live maximum, refill rate and elapsed withdrawal times remain unverified.

Hashi describes roughly five minutes from submission as one trigger for building a batch. The full withdrawal also depends on approval, available payout capacity, signatures and Bitcoin confirmations. Hashi’s withdrawal FAQ describes how to check current capacity. The deployed maximum and refill rate remain unverified, as does the time from repayment to a Bitcoin payout for a Fluid borrower.

On Nov. 21, Merkl’s Fluid lending campaign displayed $862.26K in total value locked in its Sui fUSDC rewards opportunity. The figure captures activity on the lender side of the market. The amount of USDC borrowers have drawn against hBTC remains unverified.

Fluid says Anchorage Digital’s institutional clients can lend USDC or borrow it against hBTC on the protocol. Anchorage Digital Bank N.A. provides custody and settlement services. The Sui Foundation says institutions can already use Anchorage’s self-custody wallet, Porto, to access hBTC and select Fluid strategies. It expects tri-party settlement in early 2027. Anchorage identifies Atlas as its tri-party route for institutions that keep assets in qualified custody.

#5026. What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

Perpetual futures are the engine room of crypto trading. Centralized exchanges processed $85.3 trillion in perps trading volume in 2025 alone, according to CoinGecko's State of Crypto Perpetuals Report 2026, and the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) describes perps as the dominant form of crypto derivatives trading.

They aren't only for crypto, either. Exchanges now offer perps on stocks, commodities and currencies, and Decrypt has become the first news publisher to offer perps trading, powered by Solana exchange Phoenix.

This guide explains what futures are, what makes perpetual futures different, how funding rates and liquidations work, and what to know before trading them.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

To understand perpetual futures, it helps to start with ordinary futures.

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a set price on a set date in the future. Futures have been used for centuries: farmers used them to lock in prices for crops before harvest, and today they are traded on everything from oil and wheat to stock indexes and Bitcoin, on regulated exchanges such as CME.

Traders use futures for two main reasons. Hedgers use them to protect against price swings; an airline might buy oil futures to lock in fuel costs, for example. Speculators use them to bet on price movements without owning the underlying asset.

Traditional futures have an expiry date. When a contract expires, it settles, either by delivering the asset or, more often, by paying out the difference in cash. Traders who want to keep their position open must "roll" it into a new contract, which can add costs and complexity.

Perpetual futures, often shortened to "perps" or "perpetual swaps," work like futures, but with one key difference: they never expire. Traders can hold a position for as long as they like, provided they have enough collateral to keep it open.

The idea was proposed by Yale economist Robert Shiller in a 1993 paper as a way to create markets for assets that are hard to trade directly. Perps took off in crypto. Crypto exchange BitMEX, widely credited with bringing perps to crypto, launched a perpetual contract on Bitcoin in 2016. BitMEX itself closed in September 2026, but the product it pioneered has become the most traded instrument in crypto.

A perpetual contract never actually involves owning the asset. Traders deposit collateral, or "margin," and their profit or loss depends entirely on how the price moves.

When opening a perp position, a trader chooses a direction. Going long means betting the price will rise; going short means betting it will fall. Being able to short as easily as going long is one reason perps are popular: it lets traders profit from falling prices, or hedge assets they already own.

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets traders control a larger position than their collateral alone would allow. With 10x leverage, for example, $100 of margin controls a $1,000 position. If the asset's price rises 5%, the trader makes $50, a 50% return on their margin. But if it falls 5%, they lose $50, half of their margin.

Exchanges track two prices to manage this. The index price is the asset's price on spot markets, usually averaged across several exchanges. The mark price, based on the index and the perp market itself, is used to calculate profits and losses and to decide when positions must be closed. Using an averaged price makes it harder for a sudden spike on one exchange to trigger liquidations.

Because perps never expire, nothing forces their price to converge with the underlying asset, as traditional futures do at expiry. Instead, perps use a funding rate.

At regular intervals—most commonly every eight hours, though some platforms use shorter or longer schedules—traders on one side of the market pay traders on the other:

Decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, for example, pays funding every hour, at one-eighth of the rate calculated for an eight-hour period, to stay consistent with centralized exchanges.

Funding payments go directly between traders, not to the exchange. For traders holding positions over long periods, these payments can add up, and they can either boost or erode returns.

Funding rates also offer a window into market sentiment. Strongly positive funding suggests traders are crowded into bullish bets, while negative funding shows that bears are paying to stay short.

Rates for the same asset can also differ between exchanges. On November 19, 2026, Bitcoin's funding rate was 0.01% per eight hours on Hyperliquid, compared with 0.0037% on Binance, according to Hyperliquid's funding comparison tool.

Some traders aim to earn funding payments rather than bet on prices. In a "cash and carry" or basis trade, they buy an asset on the spot market and short the same amount through perps, collecting funding while their positions offset each other's price moves.

Leverage is a double-edged sword. Every perp position has a maintenance margin, the minimum collateral needed to keep it open. If losses push a trader's margin below that level, the exchange automatically closes the position, a process called liquidation. The trader typically loses most or all of the margin they put up.

The higher the leverage, the smaller the price move needed to trigger a liquidation. At 50x leverage, a 2% move against a position can wipe it out.

Liquidations can also feed on each other. When prices fall sharply, liquidated longs are forced to sell, pushing prices lower and triggering further liquidations, a so-called liquidation cascade. On October 10, 2025, the crypto market saw its largest liquidation event on record, with more than $19 billion in leveraged positions wiped out in a single day.

To cover losses when positions can't be closed in time, exchanges typically keep an insurance fund. In extreme cases, some use auto-deleveraging, closing profitable positions on the other side of the market to keep the system solvent.

Crypto perps trade on two main types of venue.

Centralized exchanges, such as Binance, Bybit and OKX, run their own order books and hold users' funds. They account for the vast majority of perps trading: Binance alone held about a third of centralized perps volume in early 2026, according to CoinGecko. Many are unavailable to U.S. customers.

Decentralized exchanges let traders use perps through smart contracts, while keeping control of their funds. Some, such as Hyperliquid and Solana-based Phoenix, run order books onchain, matching buyers and sellers much like a traditional exchange. Others, such as GMX and Jupiter, let traders trade against a shared pool of liquidity provided by other users.

Decentralized perps are growing fast. Their trading volume jumped from $1.5 trillion in 2024 to $6.38 trillion in 2025, led by Hyperliquid, CoinGecko found. Decentralized exchanges' share of perps trading volume peaked at 13% in late 2025 before slipping to 10% in April 2026, while their share of open interest, the total value of open positions, reached 13.5%.

In the U.S., perps developed largely on offshore exchanges, outside the reach of the CFTC, but that has started to change. In July 2025, Coinbase introduced CFTC-regulated "perpetual-style" futures for U.S. customers, with five-year expiries and up to 10x leverage. Two months later, CFTC-regulated exchange Bitnomial launched what it called the first U.S. perpetual futures, with 25-year terms and eight-hour funding. In May 2026, the CFTC approved a Bitcoin perpetual futures contract from prediction market Kalshi and set out a framework for listing more, and in June, Kraken rolled out perps to eligible U.S. customers through Bitnomial, which its parent company had acquired.

Some of these contracts, including Kalshi's and Bitnomial's original design, have long but fixed terms of 25 years, so are not strictly perpetual. The regulator says perps can be offered on CFTC-registered exchanges under federal oversight, and has advised U.S. traders to avoid offshore venues.

The shift has been contested. CME Group, the largest U.S. futures exchange, has sued the CFTC, arguing that perps are swaps rather than futures under U.S. law. The CFTC has nonetheless continued to approve more perps platforms, starting with Backpack.

Perps are now spreading beyond crypto. Exchanges have begun listing perps tracking stocks, stock indexes, commodities such as gold and oil, and currencies, giving traders around-the-clock exposure to markets that normally close in the evening and on weekends.

Those products carry the same mechanics, and the same risks, as crypto perps. They track an asset's price without giving traders ownership of the asset itself, so a perp on a company's stock doesn't come with voting rights or dividends.

Perps can be useful tools for hedging and speculation, but they are among the riskiest products in crypto:

Decrypt users can now trade perps directly alongside the news, through the Decrypt Money Account. The service is powered by Phoenix, a decentralized exchange on Solana, and lets users go long or short on crypto, stocks and commodities.

Availability varies by region, and as with any leveraged product, traders should only risk money they can afford to lose.

#5024. Senate passes ‘Death Bets Act’ to ban prediction market contracts tied to death and war

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Death Bets Act, which now heads to the House, would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The U.S. Senate has passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination, and war.

The S.4035 bill, titled "Discouraging Exploitative Assassination, Tragedy, and Harm Betting in Event Trading Systems Act," cleared the Senate on Thursday and now heads to the House of Representatives.

Sen. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) introduced the bill in March 2026 as primary sponsor, with Sen. John Curtis (R-Utah) as a co-sponsor. The bill would amend the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA) to prohibit listing contracts related to war, death, and similar activities.

"[These contracts] create rampant opportunities for insider trading, and perhaps worst of all, they're offered with no regard for national security and, in far too many cases, even basic morality," Schiff said Thursday.

Schiff added that prediction markets are operating in a "Wild West" and are "desperately in need of regulation and guardrails."

Under the CEA, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission has the authority to block contracts tied to war, terrorism, or assassination. While the final decision is at the agency's discretion, the lawmakers backing the Death Bets Act argued that such flexibility is no longer sufficient.

Prediction market platforms have also taken steps to remove controversial contracts. Earlier this year, Polymarket took down a market that let users bet on the likelihood of a nuclear weapon detonation due to criticism on social media.

The congressional push adds to the mounting legal pressure prediction market platforms are already facing. More than a dozen states have taken legal or enforcement actions against Kalshi for allegedly violating state gambling laws.

Meanwhile, federal regulators and state authorities remained locked in escalating legal battles over who has the authority to regulate prediction market platforms. The CFTC has maintained that it has jurisdiction over prediction markets under the CEA, while several states have argued that certain event contracts violate state gambling laws.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said during a Thursday interview at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum that the agency would work with the Justice Department to defend its jurisdiction in legal disputes.

In recent weeks, the CFTC and the DOJ have filed lawsuits against several states, including Utah, Arkansas, and Louisiana, seeking to block state enforcement against prediction market platforms.

"If it's a legal and lawful derivative, it should be regulated by the market regulator. It's not something that goes to the state," Selig said.

#5023. Solana’s new memory checks could make some older app transactions fail

A replay of mainnet traffic exposed one way older Anchor code can fail. The checks still await mainnet activation, and a separate change would cut the work validators do to copy data.

Solana’s planned memory checks could make some app transactions fail even when the rest of the app keeps working. Developers have already seen one example in replayed mainnet transactions. Older code built with the Anchor framework could try to save an account’s data after handing control of that account to another program.

Anza, which develops Solana’s Agave validator software, resumed general mainnet feature activations on November 19. Its November 20 feature tracker lists the memory checks and a separate change called account data direct mapping as pending, with no mainnet activation epoch or slot for either. Direct mapping is designed to spare validators the work of repeatedly copying program data.

Anza’s November 20 tracker listed both switches as pending on Solana mainnet, with no activation slot for either.

The checks can reject a write to an account the program no longer owns, even while validators still use copied data.

The paths show conditional runtime behavior. Their lengths carry no timing or performance measurement.

Conceptual diagram from SIMD-0460, the original runtime proposal and Agave’s implementation split. The same check branches to a rejected access or to two possible routes for an allowed access.

The paths show conditional runtime behavior. Their lengths carry no timing or performance measurement.

The memory checks can reject a read that runs past the end of an account or a write to an account the program cannot modify. They also change the stack layout older programs use to hold temporary data. Code that relied on that layout can behave differently after the change. One documented failure involves how Solana programs hand accounts to one another within a transaction.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

In the case described in an Anchor patch, one program calls another and transfers ownership of an account during the call. When the first program finishes, older Anchor code tries to write the account data back even when the bytes have stayed the same. The old runtime let that attempt pass. Under Solana’s new rules, the attempt fails because the first program has already given up the right to write to that account.

The patch author says programs that delegated accounts this way hit the error in production replay. A transaction that takes the affected path can fail while an app’s other transactions continue to go through.

The Anchor 1.2.2 changelog includes the fix. It compares the data after ownership changes and skips the write if the bytes match. Teams maintaining affected programs can rebuild and test with the updated framework. For a program deployed through Solana’s upgradeable Loader V3, the party authorized to update the on-chain code must deploy that build. An app team that relies on someone else’s program needs that program’s operator to make the update.

Anza engineer Alexander Meißner wrote in the original runtime proposal that the team replayed mainnet traffic. The proposal says most developers whose apps would have failed in that exercise were contacted and had made fixes. Replay checks the instruction paths that appeared in the sample. A rarely used path can escape that check if the transactions Anza replayed never took it.

Meißner’s January 30 memory rules proposal says developers of the Agave and Firedancer validator clients split the original plan so earlier work could ship even if a later part needed more time. In Agave’s implementation discussion, Meißner described a separate testing problem. Combining the new rules and the copy change made it hard to tell intended changes in execution from mistakes. Giving the two changes separate switches let developers test the new rules with the old copying path still in place.

In the old execution path, the validator copies account data into a program’s virtual machine and takes it back when the program finishes. A call to another program adds another round trip. Meißner’s proposal describes up to four copies for an instruction with a single cross-program call. With direct mapping enabled, the virtual machine can access those account bytes where they already reside.

Anza’s tracker lists the memory checks and the mapping switch one epoch apart on both testnet and devnet. On mainnet, the date to watch for affected apps is the activation of the memory checks. Validators get the copy savings when the mapping switch goes live.

#5021. Block Says Most Bitcoin Payments on Square Are for Coffee and Food

Most bitcoin payments at Square sellers are small, everyday purchases at coffee shops and restaurants, Block said in a new report that it hopes will inform a debate in Congress over a tax break for small crypto purchases.

The report, “The State of Bitcoin Payments,” covers completed bitcoin payments at U.S. Square sellers from Nov. 1, 2025, to Oct. 15, 2026. The median payment was about $13, and the average was about $29.

Food and drink businesses took 72% of those payments, Block found. That is a bigger share than the category’s 67.5% slice of regular card payments on Square over the same period.

“Most bitcoin payments are small and routine,” the company wrote on X Thursday, calling the report one of the first datasets on how people spend bitcoin in the local economy.

Coffee shops and fast food businesses accounted for 62.6% of the bitcoin payments in the food and drink category, with a median payment of $9.10. Grocery stores made up a smaller share but had the highest average payment in the group, at $60.73.

Small tickets dominated. About 42% of bitcoin payments on Square were under $10, and 65.1% were under $20. Those sub-$20 payments made up just 19.1% of total payment value, though, with a few larger purchases accounting for much of the dollars spent.

Customers also came back. Among Cash App users who paid with bitcoin at least twice at the same merchant, the median was three purchases per customer-merchant pair. About 44% of bitcoin payments from Cash App were repeat payments, and 86% of those came within 30 days.

The activity was not limited to Block’s own app. External Lightning wallets such as Strike, Wallet of Satoshi, Phoenix and Coinbase handled 56.6% of Square bitcoin payments and about 66% of payment value, while Cash App wallets made up the rest.

Square sellers can choose how much of each sale they keep in bitcoin, and Square converts the rest to dollars automatically. Block said zero-fee processing for bitcoin payments on Square has been extended through Dec. 31, 2027. Local governments are testing similar setups, with Miami-Dade County now taking bitcoin for property taxes and converting it to dollars.

Block tied the data to tax policy. Under current U.S. federal rules, paying with bitcoin generally means calculating a capital gain or loss on every purchase, even a $10 coffee, the company said.

A Morning Consult survey that Block commissioned found that 23.6% of businesses that don’t accept bitcoin cite tax and accounting complexity as a reason. That is about the same share that cite regulation, refunds or price swings. Among businesses that have considered taking bitcoin but haven’t, the figure was 27%.

Consumers pointed to friction, too. Morning Consult surveyed 8,669 U.S. adults who are aware of bitcoin in August. Among those who don’t own it, 29.6% said they would be more likely to pay with bitcoin if it were easier, and 15.7% of that group said they were unsure how it is taxed or regulated.

Block said Congress is weighing a crypto tax package and pointed to a de minimis exemption, which would exempt small gains on qualifying purchases, as one option. Industry groups have pressed lawmakers on crypto tax rules for months, with little movement so far.

The report doesn’t give totals. Block said the number of Square bitcoin payments, the number of sellers receiving them and total bitcoin payment volume are proprietary and not disclosed. Merchant lists like the 425 Los Angeles businesses that accept bitcoin offer a rough sense of reach.

“We think tax policy should account for how people spend bitcoin as well as how they hold it,” Block said.
