#5020. Protesters Arrested Outside Nasdaq as Activists Oppose Anthropic IPO Over AI Bubble Risks

Protesters Arrested Outside Nasdaq as Activists Oppose Anthropic IPO Over AI Bubble Risks

At least four protesters have been arrested outside Nasdaq’s Times Square headquarters Thursday as Anthropic celebrated its $81.34 billion initial public offering, according to a press release shared with Decrypt by organizers of the #StopTechOligarchs demonstration.

The Stop Funding Billionaires campaign accused Anthropic and its early investors of benefiting from an AI boom that could leave ordinary retirement savers exposed if share prices tumble. Organizers called on pension funds and other large investors to stay away from the company's stock.

“Teachers, firefighters, civil servants will pay for that loss,” said Jose Gonzalez, an activist with Stop Funding Billionaires and New York Communities for Change, in a statement. He argued that workers have little control over how large institutions invest the savings they depend on for retirement.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The concern centers on faster rules for adding new companies to major stock indexes. Under Nasdaq’s fast-entry policy, an eligible IPO large enough to rank among the Nasdaq-100’s top 40 companies can enter the index after 15 trading days, allowing index-tracking funds to buy the shares sooner than under older rules.

That does not mean all pension funds or 401(k) plans must purchase Anthropic shares, let alone buy them during the IPO. Funds tracking a benchmark that admits Anthropic would normally adjust their holdings, while other managers can make different choices.

Protesters also raised concerns about AI-driven job displacement, the water and energy used by data centers, and AI applications in surveillance and military targeting. Their claims about specific military and law-enforcement harms were allegations made by the campaign, not findings established by the release.

“I’m proud to join activists and community members out here today to stand against Anthropic and the tech billionaires who put profit over people,” said New York state senator-elect Aber Kawas in the organizers’ statement. Kawas also criticized AI’s military uses and the prospect of companies replacing workers with automated systems.

One protester identified by organizers as among those arrested called for a freeze on what they described as reckless AI development. “Don’t bail out Big Tech when their bubble inevitably pops,” the protester said.

Anthropic priced 980 million shares at $83 apiece, raising $81.34 billion in the initial offering. That exceeded SpaceX’s original $75 billion IPO sale, although SpaceX’s final offering was larger after underwriters bought additional shares.

The IPO also followed a sharp change in market expectations. Earlier this month, an AI-stock sell-off had led some investors to question whether Anthropic could command a valuation above $1 trillion, before demand pushed its IPO price higher.

The protesters had called for Anthropic to halt its IPO, even as the offering went ahead. The campaign is also urging pension funds, university endowments and other institutional investors not to buy shares in companies it says undermine workers’ rights.

#5018. MoonPay applies for national trust bank charter to expand stablecoin services

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

MoonPay plans to offer custody and stablecoin services through the proposed bank, subject to OCC approval.

Crypto payments firm MoonPay announced Thursday it had filed an application with the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to launch MoonPay National Trust Bank.

The application, dated Nov. 17 and received by the OCC on Nov. 18, said MoonPay's trust bank would provide fiduciary custody services; stablecoin services, including issuance, redemption, and reserve management; and conversion services as an agent.

The filing is to organize MoonPay National Trust Bank as a non-depository national bank, limited to trust company operations and related activities. It would not seek federal deposit insurance and would operate as a member bank of the Federal Reserve.

If approved, the national charter would provide a single federal framework for MoonPay to offer custody and stablecoin-related services. MoonPay’s application comes ahead of the GENIUS Act taking effect on Jan. 18, 2027, when payment stablecoin issuers will generally need approval under the new framework.

Several other major crypto firms have received full or conditional approval for the same type of OCC national trust bank charter.

Last December, the OCC granted conditional approvals to Circle (CRCL), Ripple, BitGo, Fidelity Digital Assets, and Paxos. Circle received final approval in July 2026, while Ripple received its full approval in October. Coinbase's (COIN) became effective earlier this month. Additional firms, including Stripe's Bridge, Crypto.com, and World Liberty Financial, hold conditional approvals.

Meanwhile, MoonPay has expanded its regulated and enterprise footprint over the past year. It received its New York BitLicense in June 2025 and its New York trust charter in November 2025.

Earlier this year, MoonPay agreed to acquire private markets platform North Capital in an all-stock deal. It launched enterprise stablecoin services in November 2025 and partnered with WisdomTree to incorporate a tokenized Treasury fund into stablecoin reserves. It has also announced a strategic collaboration with Dunamu, the parent company of South Korean crypto exchange Upbit, to explore stablecoin-based global payments and fund movement.

Former Commodity Futures Trading Commission Acting Chair Caroline Pham also joined MoonPay as chief legal officer and chief administrative officer last December.

#5015. Ark buys $90 million worth of Anthropic shares on debut, trims Ethereum and Solana staking ETF holdings

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Ark Invest trimmed its Ethereum and Solana staking ETF exposure while buying nearly 1 million shares in Anthropic on Thursday.

Cathie Wood-led Ark Invest bought 934,968 Anthropic shares, worth $90.9 million at Thursday's closing price, across six of its exchange-traded funds on its Nasdaq debut while offloading some of its Ethereum and Solana staking ETF holdings.

Some 60% of Ark's Anthropic buys were for its ARKK Innovation ETF, though they only represent around 0.4% of that fund's total holdings, according to the firm's latest disclosures, which are topped by $2.3 billion worth of Tesla shares.

More specifically, it sold $2.9 million worth of its 3iQ Ethereum staking ETF holdings from its ARKW Next Generation Technology fund and ARKF Blockchain & Fintech Innovation. It also offloaded $1.2 million in 3iQ Solana ETF shares from the same funds.

Ark's investment strategy aims to let no individual holding take up more than 10% of a fund's portfolio to maintain diversification. Ark is likely to continue rebalancing its fund weightings if the value of certain shares rise or fall significantly compared to other holdings.

Anthropic's (ANT) shares opened at $111.50 in its Nasdaq debut on Thursday, 34% above the $83 initial public offering price, pushing its market value as high as $2.1 trillion to top SpaceX's June listing. That could rise to about $93.5 billion if underwriters fully exercise their option to buy another 147 million shares. A planned $26 billion private placement with AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC would bring the combined total to about $107.3 billion before any exercise of the underwriters' option.

Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, told The Block the IPO was "historic," citing its record raise and Anthropic's first-day gain. However, he also said the stock's retreat from its intraday high "could be considered slightly bearish" as the broader market sold off.

Anthropic is the first frontier AI company to go public, with OpenAI also eyeing a listing in 2027.

Meanwhile, the Toronto Stock Exchange-listed 3iQ Ethereum staking ETF closed up 0.4% on Thursday at $6.85, with its 3iQ Solana ETF counterpart trading down 0.3% for the day, per TradingView. The underlying cryptocurrencies, ETH Ethereum (ETH) and SOL Solana (SOL), fell 1.1% and 0.7%, respectively, on Thursday, according to The Block's prices page.

#5014. Bitquery counted 400 fewer BTC in Maple’s disclosed wallets than Maple reported

Bitquery found 97.6% of the Bitcoin Maple reported for syrupUSDC on Nov. 18. It put the value of collateral it could count at about 152% of outstanding loans.

Maple Finance’s disclosed Bitcoin addresses held 400 fewer BTC than the lender reported for its syrupUSDC stablecoin lending pool in a Nov. 18 snapshot, according to the blockchain data firm Bitquery. Bitquery counted 15,961 BTC at those addresses against the 16,361 BTC Maple reported through its public API. That accounted for 97.6% of the reported Bitcoin.

In its investigation, Bitquery estimated that the collateral it could account for across assets was worth about 152% of the roughly $847 million in syrupUSDC loans at that point. Bitquery reported that the disclosed holdings of Ether, wrapped Bitcoin and XRP matched Maple’s figures in the same check. Bitquery read loan balances from Maple’s smart contracts and checked the totals against the pools’ books. It calculated coverage against outstanding syrupUSDC loans alone.

Depositors receive syrupUSDC when they supply USDC. Their yield comes from loans to institutional borrowers, who pledge crypto as collateral that can be sold to recover money if they stop paying.

Bitquery’s Nov. 18, 2026 snapshot measured Bitcoin balances at disclosed addresses and estimated the value of all collateral it could count against syrupUSDC loans.

The 400 BTC gap concerns balances at disclosed addresses. The coverage estimate compares assets counted across the pool with its outstanding loans.

Balances and loan coverage reflect the Nov. 18 snapshot. The address difference does not identify who controlled the coins or establish whether the discrepancy persisted. The 152% estimate applies to the pool in aggregate.

The first bar splits Maple’s reported 16,361 BTC into 15,961 BTC at listed addresses and a 400 BTC difference. Dividing 15,961 by 16,361 and rounding to one decimal gives 97.6%. The second bar plots Bitquery’s approximate 152% coverage estimate with a 100% loan-balance marker.

Balances and loan coverage reflect the Nov. 18 snapshot. The address difference does not identify who controlled the coins or establish whether the discrepancy persisted. The 152% estimate applies to the pool in aggregate.

Bitquery took the reported amounts and wallet list from Maple’s public API on Nov. 18 and checked balances on Bitcoin, Ethereum and the XRP Ledger. Four of the 26 Bitcoin addresses it checked were empty. Bitquery said coins from three had moved to an address outside the published list. The published amounts and addresses came from Maple. The blockchain data gave Bitquery a separate record of the balances at those addresses.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Bitquery published the investigation on Nov. 19 and said balances could have changed between Maple’s update and its read. The report leaves open how much of the difference those transfers explain and who controls the receiving wallet.

Maple Finance’s syrupUSDC dashboard showed about 14,162 BTC on Nov. 21 and listed 35 collateral wallets across several assets. The later dashboard leaves the Nov. 18 gap unexplained and does not establish whether Maple added the receiving address.

Maple’s dashboard also links to a automated LedgerLens check by The Network Firm Tech & Services, which reported about $1.316 billion in syrupUSDC collateral at 01:00 UTC on Nov. 17. That check predates Bitquery’s snapshot. The provider says the service relies on Maple and third-party data, including custodians and on-chain records, and offers no audit opinion or assurance. The verification dashboard reports a dollar total for the pool at the time shown. It does not break that figure down by wallet.

Published addresses give depositors a way to check what the wallets held at a given time. Establishing who controls the coins and what Maple could sell if a borrower defaults requires evidence about custody and the loan terms.

#5012. Bitcoin Price Crashes to $59,000 as Market Selloff Deepens

The bitcoin price crashed below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since early July, falling about $1,800 in roughly an hour as a deepening U.S. tech stock selloff dragged risk assets lower.

Bitcoin traded near $61,400 at 9:40 a.m. ET and slid to about $59,600 by 10:45 a.m., a drop of nearly 3%, according to Look Into Bitcoin data. The bitcoin price is down about $3,400, or more than 5%, from the same time Thursday, when it traded near $63,000.

The break of $60,000 is a key psychological level, and it came with little warning. Bitcoin price had held above $61,000 for most of the morning, even after a third straight daily loss.

Wall Street opened sharply lower. The Nasdaq Composite fell more than 2% in morning trading as shares of Anthropic extended losses in its second day since a record Nasdaq debut. Nvidia and AMD also traded lower.

Bitcoin has been moving with tech stocks. The market has questioned whether heavy spending on artificial intelligence will pay off, and Friday’s open did nothing to ease that worry.

Rate expectations are adding pressure. Traders see a roughly 94% chance the Federal Reserve raises rates in December, CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed. Higher rates tend to hurt bitcoin and other assets that pay no yield.

Earlier Friday, South Korea’s Kospi index fell more than 5% and chipmakers sank across Asia. That selling carried into U.S. trading and then into bitcoin.

Some analysts had flagged $60,000 as a floor. Katie Stockton of Fairlead Strategies told CNBC the bottom of bitcoin’s trading range sits “just below 60,000,” and that it “appears to be trying to find a footing.” Friday’s drop tests that view.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill said this week the drop is a “continuation of the existing bear market.” His base case is range trading around $60,000 to $90,000 through much of 2027.

JUST IN: Bitcoin falls below $60,000 for the first time since June.BTC is now less than 4% away from a new cycle low 👀 pic.twitter.com/GSZkbGdzZz

Funds have been sellers. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) lost $541.1 million on Thursday, its biggest one-day outflow on record. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $582 million in total, extending a streak of heavy withdrawals this week.

Leveraged traders were hit as well. More than $700 million in crypto positions were liquidated in the 24 hours before the latest drop, and about 86% were bets on higher prices. The slide below $60,000 likely forced out more of them.

The move extends a month-long slump. Bitcoin has dropped roughly $20,000 in the past month, a slide tracked as the price fell under $62,000 on Thursday.

Crypto stocks fell even faster. Strategy (MSTR) was down about 7% near $79.85, a day after it tumbled for a sixth straight session. Coinbase (COIN) fell about 3% to $105.36. Shares of Strive (ASST) fell to $15.31 Friday morning, down 21.47%, or $4.19, over the past five days. The stock has slid steadily from near $19.50 as bitcoin price’s sell-off deepened, with its sharpest drop in Friday’s trading.

Bitcoin miners took the worst of it. MARA Holdings (MARA) dropped about 11% to $5.55. Riot Platforms (RIOT) lost about 11% to $15.85, and CleanSpark (CLSK) fell about 12% to $5.13.

Data center operators that also mine bitcoin were hit hard. IREN fell about 12% to $12.62, and Cipher Digital (CIFR) dropped 14% to $5.58. Both have traded with AI stocks, which are also under pressure.

The bitcoin price is now at its lowest since the first days of July. Bitcoin set its record above $126,000 in October 2025, so it has lost more than half its value from that peak.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,600.

#5010. Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin Enters Death Cross as Anthropic IPO Fails to Lift Wall Street

Bitcoin traded around $62,640 Thursday, down 0.6% on the day, as Anthropic's record-breaking stock market debut failed to reverse a broader selloff in risk assets.

The cryptocurrency has lost roughly 28% from its late-September peak near $87,000, and doesn’t seem on its way to stop anytime soon.

Anthropic began trading on Nasdaq under ANT after pricing 980 million shares at $83, raising $81.34 billion. Its stock opened at $111.50, up 34%, and briefly pushed the Claude maker's market value past $2 trillion, as Decrypt reported.

The enthusiasm stopped at Anthropic's ticker.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

By 12:18 p.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite was down 1.55%, the S&P 500 had lost 1.59%, and the Dow was off 2.03%. Investors remain concerned about expensive AI infrastructure and whether the spending will deliver adequate returns. The weak reaction beyond Anthropic undercut hopes that a blockbuster IPO could revive appetite for beaten-down technology stocks.

Crypto's weakness predates Thursday's listing. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs have shed roughly $2.9 billion this month, including $306.4 million Wednesday, amid rising Treasury yields and expectations of further Federal Reserve rate hikes.

Some traders argue the IPO has diverted capital from crypto, but that remains unproven. As Decrypt previously noted, Bitcoin and AI stocks have declined together, suggesting a wider retreat from risk rather than a simple rotation into Anthropic shares.

Bitcoin's daily chart remains decisively bearish. At around $62,640, BTC trades below both its 50-day and 200-day exponential moving averages (EMAs), with the shorter average beneath the longer one.

This is a bearish alignment commonly associated with an EMA-based death cross. Traditionally, a death cross occurs when the 50-day simple moving average crosses below the 200-day average, signaling deteriorating shorter-term momentum. The EMA variation responds more quickly to recent prices. The crossover was confirmed today.

Momentum reinforces the picture. The relative strength index, or RSI, reads 21.3, well below the 30 threshold traders associate with oversold conditions. That can precede a relief rally, but oversold assets can keep falling.

The average directional index, or ADX, stands at 41.2. ADX measures the strength of a trend, not its direction, with readings above 25 generally indicating a strong trend. Here, the negative directional indicator exceeds its positive counterpart, confirming sellers have the advantage. A high ADX reading alone does not mean a reversal is imminent, even with RSI already deep in oversold territory.

Bitcoin has also been making lower highs and lower lows since September, with November's sharp drop slicing through several support levels. A bullish recovery would first need to reclaim $66,512, followed by resistance around $69,292 and $71,539. Above those levels, $73,785 and then $76,984 would present tougher tests of the prevailing bearish structure.

On the downside, the chart marks immediate support near $62,019, almost directly beneath the current price. A decisive daily close below that level could expose the psychological $60,000 threshold, followed by the chart's next Fibonacci extension around $56,841.

The RSI leaves room for a bounce, but the EMAs and ADX show how much work buyers still face. A move through $66,512 would open a test of $69,292; a close under $62,019 would instead put $60,000 back in focus.

#5007. U.S. Treasury Secretary Pushes Back on Bigger Fed Hike

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent pushed back Friday on calls for a bigger Federal Reserve rate hike, even as bitcoin broke below $60,000 for the first time since July and stocks sank on bets that the Fed will keep tightening into a selloff.

Bessent told CNBC he would encourage the Fed not to embrace a half-point increase at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. He cited the energy supply shock and a drop in crude prices as reasons for the central bank to hold back.

He argued that one governor now backing a 50 basis point hike had wanted a similar cut within the last year. Bessent said it “seems somewhat peripatetic” to want the bigger increase only 11 or 12 months later. He did not name the governor.

“I would encourage the Fed to look forward, not backwards,” Bessent said.

The comments came two days after Fed Governor Christopher Waller told CNBC a half-point hike is probably necessary in December and that the central bank may need to “shock people.” Waller said inflation is “headed in the wrong direction, and it’s spreading out.”

Waller is not alone. Minutes of the October meeting showed nearly all officials expect a quarter-point hike in December, and two floated a larger one. Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee also declined to rule out a bigger move on Thursday.

“We’ve now had multiple months in a row of borderline disturbingly high inflation rates with little progress in the sectors where we would expect to be seeing some progress,” Goolsbee told reporters. “So everything’s always on the table.”

Not everyone wants to go bigger. New York Fed President John Williams said Tuesday that “I don’t think the situation calls for dramatic action.” St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem has suggested as many as four hikes by the spring of 2027.

The Fed raised rates in September for the first time in more than three years and held them steady in October. Inflation has stayed hot since the Iran strikes pushed up energy prices earlier this year.

Citi still expects a quarter-point increase in December. Global chief economist Nathan Sheets said Thursday he does not see a need for 50 basis points despite persistent inflation. In a note Friday, the bank wrote that “Fed officials will consequently have little appetite to hike into an equity sell-off.”

Citi expects the Fed to hold after December and begin cutting later in 2027. Traders agree on the next step: the CME Group’s FedWatch tool showed a more than 98% chance of a quarter-point hike, and the 10-year Treasury yield fell more than 10 basis points to 4.95% as investors bought bonds.

Stocks fell for another day. The Nasdaq Composite dropped more than 2% in morning trading, and the VIX volatility index topped 30, its highest level since March.

Bitcoin traded near $59,700 shortly before 11 a.m. ET, down more than 5% on the day, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro. It broke below $60,000 for the first time since early July and is on track for a seventh straight weekly loss, the longest streak since 2022.

Higher rates are a headwind for assets that pay no yield, and the selloff has reached companies that hold bitcoin. Strategy’s stock fell for a sixth straight session on Thursday, and the bitcoin price has dropped roughly 25% in a month, a slide Bitcoin Magazine tracked earlier this week.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill tied the outlook to inflation in a note dated Nov. 18. “Our base case remains range trading around USD 60k to 90k through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives monetary policy room to ease,” he said.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $59,300.

#5006. Bitcoin Core fixes a bug that can stall nodes, leaving 29.x users to upgrade

Versions 30.5 and 31.3 patch the flaw, and 32.1 already includes the fix. Support for the affected 29.x releases ended in October.

Bitcoin Core released versions 30.5 and 31.3 on Nov. 20 with a fix for a flaw that can temporarily stop a Bitcoin node from processing blocks and transactions. Operators on affected 30.x and 31.x releases can patch within their current release lines. Operators on affected 29.x releases need to move to a newer line.

Bitcoin Core rated the flaw medium severity in its security advisory, which identifies it as CVE-2026-117348. The project says a peer can trigger a temporary interruption in block and transaction processing on an affected node.

For operators running versions 30.0 through 30.4, the maintenance update is 30.5. Operators running 31.0 through 31.2 can update to 31.3.

Bitcoin Core 32.1 shipped with the fix on Oct. 27. The project calls the flaw easy to discover and says in its 32.1 release notes that it would disclose easily discoverable vulnerabilities fixed in that release once patches were available for all maintained major versions. The two maintenance updates and the public advisory came out on the same day.

The affected 29.x versions run from 29.0 through 29.5. The project’s software lifecycle schedule dates the end of 29.x support to Oct. 27, the day 32.1 shipped. Bitcoin Core says 29.x will receive no fix for this flaw. An operator on that line can move to one of the three releases the advisory identifies as fixed, including either of the November maintenance releases.

#5005. Hackers Leak Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Customer Data

Hackers Leak Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Customer Data

Three of the biggest names in payments, Visa, Mastercard and JPMorgan Chase, disclosed that some of their customers’ data was exposed after hackers breached third-party vendors, the latest jolt in a week of tech failures that has left Americans uneasy about the systems underpinning daily life.

The data was leaked Thursday by a ransomware group, according to a report from The Wall Street Journal. Crucially, all three firms said their own networks were not breached and that no payment systems were compromised.

A Visa spokesperson said no payment data such as card numbers was affected and that the incident involved a third-party service provider in the Asia-Pacific region.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Mastercard said a “limited amount of information from a subset of cardholders enrolled in a specific benefit program” was exposed, with no impact on its payment network.

JPMorgan said attackers targeted outside service providers and likely accessed marketing data, potentially including customer names and email addresses, with no disruption to customer services.

A ransomware group calling itself n0n claimed responsibility, according to cybersecurity firm SOCRadar. None of the three companies named the breached vendor.

Earlier in the week, JPMorgan customers hit problems accessing accounts and sending payments, which the bank attributed to a software update, and the issue spread into broader complaints about other banks.

Paired with the FAA temporarily grounding flights and an earlier Microsoft outage that locked people out of email, the week stoked unusual public anxiety about the fragility of critical tech. JPMorgan was explicit that the data leak had “no connection” to the earlier service disruptions.

For crypto users, the breach lands squarely within a growing danger that customer data, not just funds, is the soft underbelly of finance.

AI has supercharged both the scale and sophistication of cyberattacks, and 2026 has brought a steady drumbeat of data exposures. The record $6.3 billion Binance hack, tied to North Korea, laid bare how aggressively state and criminal actors are targeting financial infrastructure, while a wave of breaches has hit crypto firms through third-party vendors, echoing the vendor weakness at the center of this latest incident.

The payments giants have all leaned deeper into crypto and stablecoins this year, from Visa and Mastercard’s settlement programs to JPMorgan’s growing digital-asset footprint, making their security posture increasingly relevant to the onchain economy they’re racing to join.

For now, the firms say the damage is contained, but the episode adds to a mounting sense that no institution, however large, is immune.

#5003. BlackRock’s staked Ethereum ETF can delay redemptions if unstaked ETH runs short

Only authorized participants can redeem directly with the fund, in 40,000-share baskets. iShares reported about 58,000 unstaked ETH at Friday’s close.

BlackRock’s staked Ethereum ETF can make brokers wait for large redemptions if its unstaked ETH runs short. Only authorized participants redeem directly with the fund. Most shareholders trade existing shares on Nasdaq. The ETF’s net asset value per share fell 9% on Friday.

The iShares Staked Ethereum Trust ETF, which trades as ETHB, held about 58,000 unstaked ETH and 317,646 staked ETH on Nov. 20, according to its holdings file. Unstaked ETH accounted for 15.5% of its holdings, down from about 23.6% at the end of September, when the fund’s quarterly filing showed that 76.4% of its ETH was staked. The fund held less staked ETH than the 337,045 ETH it reported in September. Its staked share rose because its total ETH holdings fell faster.

A basket contains 40,000 shares. At Friday’s basket size of 514.73 ETH, the fund’s unstaked holdings were equivalent to nearly 113 baskets. Using the reported balance of 58,138.78 ETH, 112 whole baskets would fit at that size.

The sponsor targets an unstaked reserve of 5% to 30% of the trust’s ETH, a buffer it calls the Liquidity Sleeve. The trust can also hold unstaked ETH for pending orders, expenses or shareholder distributions. The amount available for any set of orders depends on commitments, rebalancing and any other liquidity the sponsor can arrange.

Investors can also get spot Ethereum exposure through BlackRock’s unstaked ETHA fund, which forgoes staking distributions.

Only authorized participants, typically broker-dealers, can create or redeem baskets with the trust. The fund’s share count changes with net basket creations or redemptions.

The fund had 29.56 million shares outstanding on Nov. 20, down from the 32.52 million shares it reported on Oct. 31. That decline was equivalent to 74 net baskets. Share counts show net activity. They cannot show how much unstaked ETH the fund used to settle orders.

Farside Investors had yet to report ETHB’s Nov. 20 net flow as of Sunday. The issuer listed Friday’s closing discount to net asset value at 0.12%, a measure of how the exchange price compared with per-share net asset value.

The fund’s prospectus allows the sponsor to require cash orders when its reserve runs low. Under stress it can delay delivery of ETH or move to cash-only redemptions. Cash settlement can also be delayed if available ETH is insufficient. An authorized participant offered delayed settlement can cancel the order or choose to wait. For outstanding delayed in-kind orders, the sponsor plans to start unstaking and prioritize orders by date.

#5000. Researchers Publish Security Analysis of Bitcoin’s Post-Quantum SHRINCS Proposal

Several researchers have published a 131-page security analysis of SHRINCS, a proposed post-quantum signature scheme for Bitcoin, and are asking the wider community to look for flaws in it. Researcher Mikhail Kudinov announced the first version Thursday in a thread on X, saying the paper reduces SHRINCS’ security to the properties of its underlying hash function.

The analysis, dated Nov. 18, estimates classical security of 121.4 to 122.7 bits for SHRINCS, though those figures rest on assumptions that include three unproven conjectures. Kudinov said the work is not finished. The team is still working on a new analysis of a property called SM-DSPR, whose bound has so far been conjectured, and on tightening a reprogramming step and improving a bound against quantum attackers.

SHRINCS is a hash-based scheme, meaning its security rests on hash functions rather than the elliptic curve math behind today’s Bitcoin signatures. It places two designs under one key: a stateful part built on FXMSS with WOTS+C, and a stateless fallback built on SLH-DSA. Because both use the same hash function, Kudinov said, the proof has to cover both.

Signature size is the main cost. Under the draft proposal, stateful SHRINCS signatures run from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while the stateless fallback is 5,777 bytes. Schnorr signatures on Bitcoin today are 64 bytes, and ECDSA signatures typically come in around 71. Signatures take up scarce block space, so larger ones mean fewer transactions per block.

The compact stateful path carries a danger. It relies on one-time keys, and if a wallet accidentally reuses one, an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal the funds. Wallet software therefore has to track the state of every key. The stateless path avoids the problem, but at a much larger size.

Jonas Nick, Blockstream’s director of research, told Isabel Foxen Duke, head of the MARA Foundation, on MARA Foundation TV that wallets must automatically fall back to the stateless path whenever a state counter could be compromised. “It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” he said. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

Nick also warned that heavy use of the larger signatures would weigh on the network. “We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

The authors are clear that the analysis needs scrutiny. Kudinov said it has not been sufficiently peer reviewed, and the project’s GitHub repository warns that the documents may contain errors and should not be the sole basis for deploying SHRINCS. “We tried to be careful, but a proof of this size can have mistakes,” he wrote, asking readers to open an issue if they find one.

The team also produced two results that are not specific to SHRINCS: a classical bound for a property called ITSR without reprogramming, and a quadratic quantum bound for undetectability. Kudinov said they also apply to SPHINCS+ and SLH-DSA, the hash-based schemes SHRINCS borrows from, and that they were published as separate papers.

The SHRINCS work lands as quantum risk climbs the agenda. A global securities regulators’ group this week warned that moving blockchains to quantum-resistant cryptography may be especially hard, and researchers recently built a post-quantum defense for the Lightning Network after Bitcoin’s first quantum-safe transaction was shown without a fork.

The draft Bitcoin Improvement Proposal for SHRINCS is also open for comment. “We need all the possible feedback — engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said of it. “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise.”

#4998. Rep. Horsford sees opening for House vote on crypto tax bill before Congress ends

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The Digital Asset Tax Certainty Act, introduced by Rep. Jason Smith, passed the House Ways and Means Committee in September and includes most provisions from the PARITY Act, Horsford said.

Rep. Steven Horsford, D-Nev., said he wants the House to take up a bipartisan crypto tax bill before this Congress ends, after speaking with Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith, R-Mo., about its prospects.

"I talked to Chairman Smith earlier this week. He agreed that we have the opportunity to get our bipartisan bill through the House," Horsford said Thursday at The Hill and The Block's Capitol Crypto Forum.

Horsford said the bill should be brought up in the House before this Congress ends, but did not give a date for a vote.

The Digital Asset Tax Certainty Act, introduced by Smith, cleared the House Ways and Means Committee by 38-5 in September. The legislation would establish federal tax rules for digital assets. Horsford said Thursday that Smith's bill took in most of the provisions from the Digital Asset PARITY Act, which Horsford developed with Rep. Max Miller, R-Ohio.

Under Smith's committee-approved bill, a person using crypto to pay a qualifying network or transaction fee of no more than $10 would not recognize a gain or loss on that payment. The bill also extends wash-sale rules to traded digital assets.

Horsford said after the committee vote in September that the bill would tax mining and staking rewards as ordinary income but still did not settle when that income should be recognized.

#4997. Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Meet SHRINCS: Bitcoin's Emerging Frontrunner for a Post-Quantum Signature

Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick, with Mikhail Kudinov and collaborators, published a security analysis on Thursday for SHRINCS, advancing an emerging frontrunner for a post-quantum signature scheme in Bitcoin.

“We need all the possible feedback—engineers, cryptographers, protocol developers,” Kudinov, a post-quantum cryptography researcher at Blockstream, said in reference to a draft of SHRINCS’ Bitcoin Improvement Proposal (BIP). “There are a lot of things to check, test, complain about, or praise."

Notably, SHRINCS is a hash-based signature scheme that leverages SHA-256 to anchor its security in battle-tested primitives that are already used by Bitcoin, representing a conservative effort to minimize the proposal’s trust assumptions.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, the scheme comes with trade-offs, most notably by introducing potential security vulnerabilities at the implementation level if misused. The scheme combines two spending paths: a small, more efficient stateful signature option and a larger, more costly stateless path with fewer security risks on the implementation side.

Although stateful signatures are compact, preserving Bitcoin’s ability to process transactions efficiently, a user can trigger a critical failure—where an attacker can forge a signature for that address and steal funds if a one-time state is accidentally reused. Because SHRINCS’ stateful path relies on one-time keys that can only safely sign a single transaction, wallet developers must carefully track each key’s state.

“It needs to be impossible for the user to behave in a way that this catastrophic event happens,” Nick told Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “Wallet developers need to deal with this stateful component correctly.”

According to Nick, for SHRINCS to work safely in the real world, its specification and corresponding wallet implementations must automatically enforce the stateless path fallback in any situation where a state counter could possibly be compromised.

While some users might gravitate toward stateless signatures out of caution, Nick warns that widespread adoption could bloat the blockchain and drive up fees across the network.

Bitcoin’s current ECDSA signatures are typically 71 bytes long (and Taproot Schnorr signatures are 64 bytes). Under SHRINCS, stateful signatures are anywhere from 548 to 4,619 bytes, while stateless ones are 5,777 bytes. That said, optimizations of both signature paths have been accelerating with the support of AI-based research, Nick noted.

“We don’t know what the fee market will look like once we have these very big signatures,” he said. “And it’s not just the fee market, it’s also just fewer transactions fit into a block, meaning fewer people can use Bitcoin. And I think this changes the value proposition of Bitcoin.”

As the SHRINCS working group—which includes researchers backed by nonprofits including Brink and OpenSats—continues to refine the proposal’s specification, Blockstream researchers stress that getting the cryptographic foundation right is only half the battle.

Whether the broader Bitcoin ecosystem is ready to embrace SHRINCS’ inherent trade-offs will depend heavily on feedback from the network’s stakeholders in the months ahead. Meanwhile, Nick and Kudinov are requesting review of the proposal and the security analysis.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#4995. SEC Freezes Assets in $560 Million Crypto Trading Ponzi Scheme

The Securities and Exchange Commission has frozen the assets of Indianapolis-based Backward Hat Capital and its two co-founders, saying the firm ran a $560 million Ponzi scheme that told investors their money would be turned into crypto and used for AI-driven currency trading.

The agency said Backward Hat, co-founders Phillip Williams and Devin Johnson, Johnson’s firm Traders Point Capital and Joseph Anderson raised the money from more than 2,000 investors starting in July 2022. A federal court granted a temporary restraining order, an asset freeze and expedited discovery to halt what the SEC called an ongoing scheme.

Federal prosecutors in New York and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission brought parallel cases. The SEC and CFTC suits were filed in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Indiana, and the CFTC said the court has appointed Kevin B. Duff as temporary receiver to take control of the companies and the individual defendants’ assets.

According to the SEC’s complaint, Williams and Johnson sold limited partnership interests in private funds through a network of promoters, including to Williams’s former investment advisory clients. They promised a proprietary, “AI-driven” algorithm that supposedly returned at least 6% every month by trading foreign exchange.

Investor money, the pitch went, would first be converted into a crypto asset and then used for that arbitrage. The complaint does not name bitcoin as the asset. It says about $109 million of the roughly $210 million raised from July 2022 to September 2025 was sent to the fund’s Coinbase account and converted.

Of that, more than $56 million went to personal crypto accounts held by Williams, Johnson and a relief defendant, according to the complaint. About $40 million ended up on three platforms where some of it was used to trade other crypto assets, but little to no money was used to trade FX. After October 2025, the scheme raised about $350 million, and none of it was sent to Coinbase.

The SEC said the defendants made more than $250 million in Ponzi payments to earlier investors and moved more than $220 million to themselves. The money paid for exotic cars, expensive homes, private air travel and other businesses they owned, and helped feed Johnson’s gambling. Promoters took more than $50 million.

Anderson is accused of producing fake financial reports, including at least two “audit reports” and a “quality of earnings” report from a supposedly independent firm. The SEC said the documents falsely claimed the returns had been verified against bank and brokerage records. It said Anderson did no audit work, was not independent and had a financial stake in the scheme.

Prosecutors in Manhattan charged Williams and Johnson with securities fraud, conspiracy to commit wire fraud and conspiracy to commit securities fraud. The FBI arrested Williams in Kokomo, Indiana, and Johnson in Gilbert, Arizona, on Wednesday. Prosecutors put the total raised at more than $600 million.

“As alleged, Phillip Patrick Williams and Devin Allen Johnson promised to use investor funds for profitable foreign exchange currency trading,” said U.S. Attorney Jamie McDonald. “Williams and Johnson instead stole hundreds of millions of dollars from their investors for personal expenses and to pay off earlier investors in a Ponzi-like fashion.”

The CFTC put the Ponzi at more than $550 million from at least 2,100 participants and said defendants kept soliciting money through at least October 2026. Its complaint seeks restitution, disgorgement, civil penalties, trading bans and a permanent injunction. The SEC is seeking injunctions, disgorgement and civil penalties.

The case follows other recent enforcement actions tied to crypto-branded fraud. The SEC charged two companies in a $130 million crypto trading scheme last month, and New York sought a $1.37 billion judgment against the operators of the NovaTech Ponzi scheme. In August, the CFTC charged Goliath Ventures with a $400 million bitcoin fraud.

Anne McKinley, acting associate director of the SEC’s Chicago Regional Office, said the promise of “a low-risk, high rate of return on a purportedly complex investment should make all potential investors stop and think before investing their money.”

#4989. JPMorgan says bitcoin price correction widens gap to estimated $86,000 production cost

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

JPMorgan says the widening gap likely put more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin reserves.

Bitcoin's recent price drop has widened the gap between its market price and JPMorgan's estimated production cost of $86,000, likely putting more pressure on higher-cost miners to sell bitcoin from their reserves.

Bitcoin was trading near $65,000 when JPMorgan analysts led by Nikolaos Panigirtzoglou published the report on Wednesday, about $21,000 below their central cost estimate. Bitcoin fell below $60,000 on Friday for the first time since July, widening that gap to more than $26,000.

The analysts said miners likely added to the selling as the gap widened. Higher-cost miners may have sold their bitcoin reserves to raise cash, continue operating or fund a move into AI, they said.

"That said, we believe the gap could narrow as the hashrate declines and mining difficulty adjusts as unprofitable miners exit the network," the analysts wrote.

The analysts said crypto prices fell sharply over the past two weeks as tech stocks slid further and investors revised their expectations for Fed rates.

Spot bitcoin and ether ETFs, along with leveraged ETFs linked to Michael Saylor's Strategy, saw large outflows in both October and November, similar to May and June, the analysts said. Weekly data showed that the outflows were heavier in the latest two weeks than in the previous four weeks. Equity ETFs, on the other hand, saw inflows.

The analysts said retail investors likely drove most of the crypto ETF outflows because institutional positioning in futures markets largely held up. "Our CME futures positioning proxies suggest that bitcoin positioning has declined modestly, while ethereum positioning remained relatively elevated, holding up even better than bitcoin," the analysts said.

As for retail investors, they may have sold crypto ETFs to fund stock ETF purchases, seeing better opportunities to buy the dip in equities, according to the analysts. They also pointed to a decline in stablecoin market capitalization as a sign that "some capital has also left the crypto market altogether."

#4987. Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Nvidia, AMD, Micron Sink as Anthropic Closes 17% Above IPO Price

Anthropic made stock market history Thursday, raising $81.34 billion in the largest initial IPO share sale ever. But Wall Street's response was considerably less enthusiastic than the demand for the Claude developer's shares.

The stock opened at $111.50 on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT, a 34% premium over its $83 offering price, briefly pushing the company's fully diluted valuation past $2 trillion.

The rally faded quickly, however, with shares dropping toward $93 before closing today at $97.17.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The offering involved 980 million shares and surpassed SpaceX's original $75 billion IPO in June. As Decrypt previously reported, SpaceX's expanded final offering reached $86.25 billion, meaning Anthropic has not surpassed that completed deal.

Investors initially welcomed the listing, sending technology stocks higher before the opening bell. By the afternoon, however, the Nasdaq Composite was down 2.06%, the S&P 500 had fallen 1.9%, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average was off 2.17%.

The selloff extended across the AI supply chain. A Thursday afternoon market heatmap showed Nvidia falling 3.4%, Broadcom losing 2.07%, and memory chipmaker Micron slipping 2.17%. Microsoft was down 1.97%, while cloud infrastructure and software provider Oracle declined 2.97%.

AMD was among the hardest hit, down 7.73% in the day, although its decline had a separate catalyst. Reuters reported that the chipmaker came under pressure after investigative outlet Hunterbrook disclosed that its affiliated investment fund had taken a short position in the company.

The picture was not uniformly negative. Palo Alto Networks rose 6%, CrowdStrike gained 2.24%, and Intel added 2.25% in the same snapshot. The divergence shows that investors were distinguishing between companies rather than selling every technology stock indiscriminately.

The broader weakness comes as investors question the cost of building AI infrastructure and the returns companies can generate from that spending. Nvidia's latest earnings beat expectations, but uncertainty following Jensen Huang's resignation has continued to weigh on the semiconductor giant.

Anthropic also faces an expensive expansion. Its IPO disclosures detailed approximately $900.9 billion in infrastructure and computing commitments extending through 2045, alongside another $150 billion to $175 billion in estimated payments under less precisely defined arrangements.

"Anthropic's record-breaking IPO has failed to rekindle enthusiasm for tech stocks," Joe Maher, market economist at Capital Economics, told Reuters.

Nvidia's weakness was notable given its latest results. The chipmaker reported $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue on Tuesday, up 103% from a year earlier, and forecast stronger-than-expected sales for the following quarter.

Even those numbers failed to erase concerns over the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang after allegations of extramarital affairs and possible leaks of corporate secrets and the sustainability of the AI spending boom.

The pressure also reached companies that rent out computing capacity. Neocloud operators CoreWeave and Nebius fell 5.0% and 7.8%, respectively. Software companies were weak as well: Salesforce declined 3.17% and Adobe lost 2.03% in the afternoon heatmap. From chips and data centers to enterprise software, the losses extended beyond any single corner of the AI trade.

Anthropic's IPO prospectus shows how closely these companies are tied together. Nvidia and Google each committed roughly $10 billion to a conditional private share placement alongside the IPO.

AMD agreed to invest $2.5 billion; Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft committed $1 billion apiece; and TSMC pledged $500 million. The $26 billion placement is separate from the public offering. Several of these investors are also key suppliers or infrastructure partners in the AI ecosystem.

That creates a central question for investors: How much of the money flowing into AI companies will ultimately translate into profitable demand for chips, cloud capacity and software?

Anthropic's blockbuster debut demonstrates demand for its shares, but Thursday's broader decline shows that Wall Street is not ready to extend that confidence to every company financing or supplying the AI boom.

#4983. Binance hack laundering ramps up as $149 million in bitcoin mixed through coinjoins: Elliptic

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Attackers moved $860 million in ether from major wallets, swapping some into bitcoin before mixing it within hours.

Launderers allegedly linked to North Korea are now mixing tens of millions of dollars in bitcoin a day in a bid to make tracing the funds harder.

At least 2,310 BTC, worth around $149 million at the time, has been mixed through coinjoin transactions, with roughly 90% of that occurring since Tuesday, Elliptic said in a Friday update.

Coinjoins are a method of obfuscating the source of a bitcoin transaction by combining different users' transactions. This makes it harder to match senders' and recipients' coins.

This follows transfers of around 488,000 ETH, valued at $860 million, from five wallets. Most of this ether is now parked in new addresses, minus around $70 million that was swapped to bitcoin through THORChain. That BTC was subsequently mixed within hours.

The report's author, Tom Robinson, noted the coinjoins were organized by Kruw, a Wasabi Wallet coordinator. Coordinators arrange the transactions to be mixed but don't take custody of users' funds.

The 18,720 BTC stolen directly from Binance are still untouched. The bitcoins being mixed stem from swaps of other stolen assets like ETH, XRP and SOL.

Despite this, Elliptic said very little has likely been cashed out. The firm's latest tally puts about $2.35 billion in wallets linked to the theft, while $2.85 billion is frozen.

Separately, Binance said Wednesday that it obtained a $6.5 billion worldwide freeze order from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts against the attackers. Additional court orders give issuers and exchanges the green light to return stolen assets that they've already frozen.

Binance previously described the October breach as a monthslong campaign involving malicious software, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that gave attackers access to the exchange's wallet-signing systems.

#4980. Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi Says Customers Are Responsible When AI Banking Agents Go Rogue—Even After a Hack

Citi has updated its online banking terms to make customers responsible for the actions of third-party AI agents accessing their accounts, including when an agent leaks their credentials and someone else uses them to get in.

The change, reported by TechCrunch Wednesday, makes explicit how the bank intends to handle an increasingly awkward question: Who pays when software handling your money makes a mistake?

Under Citi’s new terms, actions taken using credentials supplied to an outside agent are treated as authorized by the customer, even if the tool ignores instructions, exceeds its permissions, or shares those credentials with another party.

Citi says it has no obligation to investigate whether the agent followed the customer's original prompts, subject to applicable law.

A Citi spokesperson told TechCrunch that the November revision clarifies the risks of sharing banking credentials or granting outside tools account access. The provision does not apply to AI agents offered by Citi itself.

Citi is not acting alone. JPMorgan and Bank of America announced similar policies in October, while Citizens Bank warned customers this week by email that they could be responsible for what their AI assistants do.

PNC also changed its digital agreement on Wednesday. Its rules distinguish bank-approved connections, which can provide limited access, from handing an agent unrestricted access to an account.

The move follows a banking security scare from October 22. Major banks had detected AI agents logging into customer accounts, sometimes taking steps that appeared to go beyond their users' instructions, and executives said they struggled to determine which agents had been properly authorized.

An AI agent differs from a conventional chatbot because it can act on a user's behalf, potentially navigating websites, accessing financial records, or initiating payments. That makes a mistaken instruction or compromised login more consequential than a bad answer about a bank balance.

The Office of the Comptroller of the Currency has since issued AI examiner guidance that addresses outside agents, payment controls, and human approval. The framework is nonbinding and does not itself create new requirements for banks.

Citi's language does not automatically eliminate consumer fraud protections. Under U.S. federal consumer protection rules, banks generally must investigate unauthorized electronic transfers and limit customers' financial liability for fraud. Those protections cannot simply be waived through a banking agreement, although their application to transactions performed by customer-authorized AI agents remains less clear.

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau says banks cannot use agreements to waive those legal protections, although the rules distinguish theft by an outsider from transfers actually authorized by a customer or someone given access.

Whether a particular AI-related loss qualifies as unauthorized depends on how the transaction occurred.

PNC's updated agreement takes a stricter operational approach: Customers may use certain connection methods approved by the bank, but cannot give third-party AI agents their passwords or one-time security codes, and agents generally cannot initiate payments unless PNC expressly permits it.

#4977. Rep. Casten seeks to curb prediction market insider trading with DOJ whistleblower program

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Reps. Casten and Lieu introduced a bill that would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously.

Rep. Sean Casten, who could have a top spot in Congress next year, is clamping down on insider trading in prediction markets by incentivizing people to report information anonymously to the Justice Department, amid high-profile incidents involving governmental officials and military personnel.

On Friday, Casten and Rep. Ted Lieu introduced the Policing Insider Trading in Prediction Markets Act, which would create a new program at the Justice Department where whistleblowers can report information anonymously about fraud in prediction markets and could potentially be compensated if the information results in a successful enforcement action.

"Prediction markets have a serious insider trading problem, and the current Administration is engaged in corruption of epic proportions," Casten said in a statement. "By encouraging whistleblowers to come forward and report wrongdoing, we can take an important step in cracking down on insider trading to restore accountability and public confidence in the U.S. rule of law."

The news was earlier reported by Politico.

Over the past year, certain bets on prediction markets have drawn scrutiny from both Republicans and Democrats. On Thursday, the Senate passed the "Death Bets Act," which would ban prediction market contracts tied to death, terrorism, assassination and war.

Insider trading on these platforms has also emerged as an ongoing concern, particularly following reports that an active-duty U.S. Army soldier used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of the capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. Subsequent cases have raised further concerns about government officials exploiting access to nonpublic information for financial gain through prediction markets, including allegations involving the White House's former teleprompter operator.

Political candidates have likewise faced allegations of insider trading on these platforms.

With Democrats now taking control of Congress in January, prediction markets are likely to garner more scrutiny. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who is slated to chair the Senate Banking Committee, has said she would hone in on prediction markets. On the House side, it is not yet clear who will lead the House Financial Services Committee. Rep. Maxine Waters had been expected to take on that role, but has since been challenged by Casten.

#4975. CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

CryptoUK Urges FCA Not to Treat DeFi Devs as Regulated by Default

The UK's main crypto trade association has called on the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) to make sure that developers and governance participants in decentralized finance protocols are not treated as regulated firms by default.

CryptoUK set out its position in a response to the FCA's consultation paper CP26/36, which proposes guidance on the "perimeter" of the UK's new crypto regime: which activities fall inside it and need FCA authorization, and which don't. The group, which says it represents more than 150 crypto companies, submitted its response on Wednesday, November 18, the day the consultation closed, and published it a day later.

Yesterday, CryptoUK submitted its response to the Financial Conduct Authority’s Consultation Paper CP26/36, which proposes amendments to the Perimeter Guidance Manual (PERG) to reflect the FSMA 2000 (Cryptoassets) (Miscellaneous Amendments) Regulations 2026.

The consultation… pic.twitter.com/oOrZgAWscP

— CryptoUK (@CryptoUKAssoc) November 19, 2026

"Software development, maintenance, security work and participation in decentralised governance should not, by themselves, establish that a person carries on a regulated activity," CryptoUK wrote. That would be consistent with the FCA's "clear controlling person" approach to DeFi, under which regulation should hinge on who effectively controls a financial service, it argued.

CryptoUK said it "broadly" supports the draft guidance, which reflects further amendments to the UK's crypto legislation that the government laid in September. The FCA aims to publish its final guidance in January 2027. It welcomed new exclusions from the regime for temporary settlement and payment arrangements, assets backing UK-recognized stablecoins, proprietary trading and market making, and technical service providers.

But in several places, the draft "goes further than the legislation," the group said. It pointed to a proposed link to the UK based on where a protocol is developed or governed, requirements in the exclusions for payments and proprietary trading, and indicators that would narrow the exclusion for technical services. The FCA has acknowledged that the timing of the latest amendments created uncertainty for some firms, and said it will give applicants extra time where questions over the technical services exclusion held up their applications for authorization.

The group asked the FCA to publish a public record of stablecoins that qualify under the UK regime, to clarify how stablecoins differ from electronic money, and to include more examples of activities that typically fall outside the perimeter.

CryptoUK also urged the FCA to raise with the Treasury outcomes that put UK firms at a disadvantage, citing the treatment of UK technical providers that offer access to regulated overseas trading venues, and of payments using stablecoins issued abroad. The UK should align with the EU's MiCA framework "where appropriate," it said. The response was prepared with law firm CMS and signed by CryptoUK policy director Nick Brooks.

The consultation is part of the FCA's work to build out the UK's crypto rulebook. The regulator finalized its core rules for crypto firms in June, ahead of a regime due to take effect on October 25, 2027, and later issued guidance two weeks before its authorization window for crypto firms opened. It has also set out a shared vision for tokenization with the Bank of England.

The Zcash miner plans to trade as TUDE after closing its HeartSciences merger, with Nasdaq listing approval still pending.

Zcash miner Fortitude has cleared another hurdle to going public after HeartSciences shareholders approved a merger between the two.

Roughly 86% of votes from Thursday's meeting supported the deal, Fortitude said in a Friday release. Fortitude now needs to get Nasdaq listing approval. Once completed, the combined company will take on the name Fortitude Mining Group and expects to trade under the ticker TUDE. A listing date has not been set.

"Today’s vote paves the way for Fortitude to become a premier publicly traded Zcash mining platform," CEO Jaime Leverton said in a statement.

Fortitude, which spun out of Digital Currency Group's Foundry mining operations in January 2025, currently has 60 MW of power capacity across its seven U.S. sites.

Its operating Zcash hashrate metric has grown nearly 15% to 5.4 GSol/s from 4.7 GSol/s reported in early October.

Last month, Fortitude signed a letter of intent with mining hardware manufacturer Bitmain to be first in line for its next-gen Zcash miners. This included a non-binding commitment to purchase up to $100 million in equipment. That followed a July agreement to buy 9,000 of Bitmain's Z15 Pro machines for a total of $31.5 million.

Fortitude sells or monetizes all the ZEC it mines, according to Friday's release. It also expects to contribute $2 million in ZEC at the merger's closing to help fund transaction costs and other expenses.

ZEC traded around $753 on Friday, down roughly 55% from its all-time high of $1,680 reached about two months ago. Despite the pullback, it remains among the strongest performers year to date among the top 20 cryptocurrencies, alongside Hyperliquid's HYPE.

#4968. Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase Targets In-House Prediction Markets by First Half of 2027: Report

Coinbase is planning to launch its own prediction market contracts in the U.S. in the first half of 2027, as it looks to take more control of one of its fastest-growing businesses.

The exchange plans to offer the in-house event contracts to both retail and institutional customers, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing a person familiar with the matter.

A Coinbase spokesperson told Bloomberg the company had "nothing new to announce today," and pointed to plans it set out in October to expand its prediction markets and improve their economics by building its own clearing and other infrastructure. The spokesperson did not address the timing of an in-house launch.

Coinbase rolled out prediction markets across the U.S. through a partnership with Kalshi, after unveiling the product alongside stock trading last year. Offering its own contracts would give Coinbase more say over which markets it lists, and let it keep more of the revenue from trades.

The exchange has been laying the groundwork for its pivot. In December 2025, it agreed to buy prediction markets startup The Clearing Company, and in September 2026 it received approval from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) for its own clearinghouse. Coinbase already runs a regulated derivatives exchange.

Prediction markets have become a bright spot for Coinbase as crypto trading slows. The business passed $200 million in annualized revenue in the third quarter, with daily traders up more than 60% from the previous quarter, and revenue growing 82% a quarter on average since launch, the company reported in October.

"Prediction markets are adding another revenue stream and are less correlated to crypto volatility," CFO Alesia Haas said at the time. On an analyst call the same day, Haas said Coinbase's new clearinghouse meant it "can launch our own contracts, and we can also then earn better economics," while continuing to offer contracts through partners such as Kalshi. Asked when, she said: "We have not shared a timeline."

The products have faced legal challenges from states that argue some event contracts amount to unlicensed gambling. Coinbase sued three states over their attempts to regulate prediction markets, and New York sued Coinbase and Gemini over their offerings in April.

Last week, a Louisiana judge temporarily barred more than 30 prediction market providers, including Coinbase's derivatives arm, from offering event contracts in the state, after Attorney General Liz Murrill sued them on November 12, Forbes reported. The CFTC has sued Louisiana in federal court to block the order, and a hearing on a longer injunction is set for November 30.

Decrypt's parent company, Dastan, operates its own prediction market, Decrypt Predict.

#4958. Chainflip’s FLIP drops 34% after attacker drains $12 million from vaults with suspected key compromise

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

An attacker emptied the Ethereum and Bitcoin vaults of cross-chain DEX Chainflip, looting about $4qw million in crypto and sending it to Tornado Cash, apparently by using a legitimate signing key.

Cross-chain decentralized exchange Chainflip Chainflip (FLIP) paused its protocol on Saturday morning after security firms detected an attack which looted about $4.86 million in ETH and stablecoins from its Ethereum-based vault, apparently using a valid signature from the protocol's own signing key.

Chainflip later disclosed that 122.86 BTC was also stolen from its Bitcoin vault, bringing the total value of stolen crypto to about $12 million. Chainflip has offered a 10% bounty to the attacker, which it says it may be able to identify based on metadata traces they left behind.

"We're aware of a potential exploit affecting Chainflip vaults," the protocol shared on X. "As a precaution, the protocol is being put into full safe mode. Deposits, withdrawals, swaps and quoting are disabled."

Chainflip's FLIP token plummeted about 34% following the 2:10 a.m. ET exploit, per The Block's Chainflip price page. The token's market capitalization dropped from about $41.6 million to $27.1 million as of publication time.

Security firm Decurity first shared details of the attack on X through its Defimon Alerts bot. The alert noted Chainflip's Ethereum vault was drained of "its entire liquid balance," making up 520.2 ETH, 3.25M USDC and 758K USDT worth around $4.86 million at the time. The alert said that rather than exploiting a smart contract bug, the attacker apparently obtained access to the validator network's aggregate signing key, meaning the secret code that authorizes transfers.

The attacker launched the hack through a fresh wallet, and later sent nearly all the proceeds through mixing protocol Tornado Cash, per security firm AMLBot. "The laundering was executed with high-speed automation," AMLBot stated, noting that the stablecoins moved too fast for USDC issuer Circle and USDT issuer Tether to freeze the tokens, as the whole attack took place in under 10 minutes.

Chainflip has not said how the attacker may have compromised the key, which is jointly controlled by the protocol's 150 validators and rotated every three days; the last rotation happened on Nov. 18 at 4:53 p.m. ET, about two and a half days before the exploit. No one validator is supposed to have access to the full key; rather, each holds a piece and 100 out of 150 are required to sign transactions.

Blockchain data firm Bitquery noted in its investigation that Chainflip's key changed about an hour and 45 minutes after the theft. The timing suggests Chainflip rotated the compromised key to prevent further thefts, though Bitquery noted that no conclusions can be drawn from timing alone.

In another quirk Bitquery noted, the attacker's transaction carried a strange nonce, or a unique number that every Chainflip withdrawal is stamped with. That number, each of which is only used once, usually increments by one to prevent anyone from replaying old transactions, but rather than having a nonce around 315,000 like other transactions around that time, the attacker's nonce was a 39-digit number.

That suggests the approval wasn't made through Chainflip's normal system, which would have handed out the next number on the counter, but that the attacker somehow produced a valid signature outside that system. Bitquery noted that the drain was the only one of 299,677 signatures since 2023 that broke the count.

Chainflip later said the attacker also took 122.86 BTC from its Bitcoin vault, bringing total losses to about $12 million. A separate transaction moved about 10.5 BTC from a previous Chainflip vault address to a new address about two hours after Chainflip paused the protocol. That transfer is not among the assets Chainflip listed as stolen.

Chainflip previously suffered an exploit on Sept. 12 that saw an attacker abuse a flaw in how Chainflip processed Tron transaction memos to double-process six withdrawals on the Tron network.

The protocol's validators later voted to reimburse the affected liquidity providers (LPs) using the protocol's "surplus funds," which Chainflip described as "a combination of many things. It is both excess funds held by the protocol through gas fee interactions, assets sent to Chainflip in error that the protocol could not automatically return, and other sometimes so far unexplained surpluses that have built up in the vaults since launch."

Chainflip has not said whether it plans to reimburse the victims of the current hack. Chainflip did not immediately respond to a request for comment from The Block.

Updated Nov. 22 at 1:00 p.m. ET with Chainflip's disclosure of the bitcoin theft and a link to our updated reporting.

#4957. Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin ETFs Shed $582M in a Day as BTC Drops Below $62K

Bitcoin slid below $62,000 on Friday, extending a weeklong sell-off as investors pulled money from spot Bitcoin ETFs and braced for another interest rate hike from the Federal Reserve.

The leading cryptocurrency was trading at around $61,780, down 2.8% over the past day and 10% over the past week, after touching a 24-hour low of $61,273—its lowest level since early July—according to CoinGecko data. It has fallen more than $2,000 since struggling to hold $64,000 earlier this week, and has also formed a so-called death cross, a bearish chart pattern.

The drop triggered $727 million in liquidations across crypto markets over the past 24 hours, about 86% of which were long positions betting on higher prices, according to CoinGlass. Bitcoin accounted for just under $202 million of the total, with more than 151,000 traders liquidated.

Spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $582.2 million in net outflows on Thursday, their largest single-day outflow since October 30, according to SoSoValue data. The funds have lost more than $1.1 billion so far this week and almost $3.5 billion in November, while their total net assets have fallen to $76.6 billion.

"Within crypto, the most telling sign is this month's outflow from spot Bitcoin ETFs, which suggests investors are choosing to stay on the sidelines for now," Neil Steinhardt, head of Nexo U.S., told Decrypt.

Treasury yields above 5% and expectations that the Fed will raise rates in December have weighed on assets that don't pay interest, including Bitcoin and gold, Steinhardt said. "Because the pressure appears tied to the rate backdrop, and that backdrop has shifted before, a turn in yields or the dollar would likely be the first signal that appetite is returning," he added, pointing to the December 4 jobs report and the Fed's December 9 meeting as the next checkpoints.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller has called for a larger, half-point increase at that meeting, unless policymakers signal another rise in January. Traders are betting on a smaller move: CME FedWatch puts the odds of a quarter-point hike on December 9 at 99.2%, with the chance of a half-point increase falling to almost zero from more than 9% on Thursday morning.

The decline has left the average Bitcoin holder only just in profit. Bitcoin is still trading above its realized price, the average price at which every coin on the network last moved, but the margin is thin, onchain analytics firm Glassnode tweeted on Friday. A break below would put holders in loss for the first time since early 2023, it said.

The average Bitcoin holder is still in profit.

bitcoin:native trades above its realized price, the average cost of every coin on the network. The margin is thin: MVRV sits just above 1.

CoinShares head of research James Butterfill sees the slide as part of a longer downturn. "I see this as a continuation of the existing bear market rather than the start of another," he said in a note published on Thursday, pointing to selling by large holders since late 2025, expectations of higher rates and weakness in tech stocks.

CoinShares' base case is for Bitcoin to trade between $60,000 and $90,000 through much of 2027, until falling inflation gives the Fed room to ease, Butterfill said.

#4954. Cyber investigating suspected $1 million bridge exploit as Upbit, Bithumb freeze transfers

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Upbit and Bithumb froze CYBER deposits and withdrawals as tokens pulled from the Ethereum bridge for Binance Labs-backed firm Cyber, formerly CyberConnect, were sold for about $968,000 in ETH.

Cyber, the social-focused Layer 2 network formerly known as CyberConnect, said Saturday that it is investigating "unexpected minting" of its CYBER CyberConnect (CYBER) token on Solana, which was followed by a transfer of CYBER out of its bridge's Ethereum lockbox. South Korean exchanges Upbit and Bithumb suspended CYBER deposits and withdrawals shortly after noon ET, citing a suspected security incident.

In an X post at 1:25 p.m. ET, Cyber said its team is checking which assets and bridge routes are affected. It asked users to hold off on using the CYBER bridge or depositing CYBER to exchanges, and said they don't need to migrate tokens, connect wallets or sign anything.

Upbit's caution notice linked two transactions. In the first, 5,213,000 CYBER was minted on Solana at 11:13 a.m. ET. Two minutes later, the same amount left CyberTokenAdapter, the Ethereum contract that holds CYBER for the token's LayerZero bridge, and went to a wallet that had been funded for the first time less than an hour earlier.

That wallet and a second one sold most of the tokens for ETH over the next hour through KyberSwap and MetaMask Swaps, mostly in clips of 150,000 CYBER at a time. At 12:40 p.m. ET, they sent 575.29 ETH to a fresh address, where it was still sitting as of publication. Another roughly 412,000 CYBER went to three addresses that block explorers haven't labeled.

CYBER fell about 8% to $0.2024 as the tokens were sold, before recovering to about $0.213, according to The Block's Cyber price page as of publication time. Bithumb said CYBER trading would continue during the freeze, and Upbit issued a trading caution rather than halting trading.

Cyber started out as CyberConnect, a social graph protocol that raised a $10 million seed round co-led by Multicoin Capital and Sky9 Capital in 2021. The CYBER token launched through Binance Launchpool in August 2023, and Binance Labs, now known as YZi Labs, later made a follow-on investment in the project. In May 2024, CyberConnect rebranded as Cyber and launched an Ethereum Layer 2 network for social apps built on Optimism's OP Stack, spinning off its Link3 app as a separate brand.

Earlier this month, Cyber said it had bought back about $13 million worth of CYBER over the past year. The Block has reached out to Cyber for further comment.
